#2840. Demis Hassabis’ AI company raises $5 billion, months after he left as Google DeepMind CEO

Isomorphic Labs, an artificial intelligence drug discovery startup founded by former Google DeepMind chief Demis Hassabis, has raised a $5 billion Series C, the company said on Tuesday.

Isomorphic's round comes three-and-a-half months after Hassabis stepped back as CEO of DeepMind, Google's AI lab, which he co-founded before the Big Tech giant acquired it in 2014.

The round was co-led by Greenoaks and Sequoia Capital, alongside Andreessen Horowitz, with investment from Alphabet, Google's parent. The fresh funds will help fund the design and development of new medicines using AI.

Drug discovery startups have seen record funding poured into them by investors in 2026 as companies look to capitalize on advances in AI. OpenAI and Anthropic have also been investing in tools to develop new medicines using the technology.

Hassabis has been CEO of Isomorphic Labs since the startup spun out of DeepMind in 2021.

"When we founded Isomorphic, our conviction was that biology could be understood and modelled at the most fundamental molecular level and radically accelerated through AI," said Hassabis.

"This round enables us to accelerate our progress: building foundational biology models, deepening our systemic disease understanding, and driving our first therapeutic programs towards clinical trials to deliver tangible impact for patients," he added.

Isomorphic is building a proprietary drug design engine and therapeutic candidate pipeline designed with AI, which it says will help it deliver the next generation of medicines.

Anthropic announced it was launching an AI drug discovery program in June and OpenAI partnered with Novo Nordisk in April.

Drug discovery startups have raised $23.2 billion in 2026, according to deal-counting platform Dealroom, a record figure.

Isomorphic last raised in May, with a $2.1 billion Series B.

#2838. Top Wall Street analysts suggest these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Inflation data, earnings reports and tensions in the Middle East have been influencing the market. In this volatile backdrop, investors seeking income can bolster their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

The recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help inform investors in their search for attractive stocks from the vast universe of dividend-paying companies. These experts assign ratings after analyzing a company's financials and ability to consistently pay dividends while also seeking capital appreciation.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Exxon Mobil has increased its dividends for 43 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.03 per share (annualized dividend of $4.12), XOM stock offers a yield of 2.6%.

In his latest research report on energy players, Morgan Stanley analyst Devin McDermott reiterated a buy rating on Exxon Mobil stock and increased his price target to $177 from $168.

The analyst raised his free cash flow estimates to reflect Q2 guidance and the oil price strip, with the largest increase noted for integrated players due to higher refining margins. Notably, the five-star analyst raised his cash flow estimates by an average of 20% for the second half of 2026 and 7% for 2027.

McDermott highlighted that while pure-play refiners have rallied on stronger margins, integrated energy stocks have lagged, creating relative-value opportunities in major North American integrated producers, including Exxon Mobil, Suncor and Cenovus Energy.

Overall, McDermott is bullish on Exxon Mobil, as he expects the company to deliver strong earnings and cash flow growth, driven by robust upstream volumes, high-value projects in Product Solutions, and more than $5 billion of additional structural cost savings.

"XOM's large global integrated operations and strong balance sheet (0.0x 2027 net leverage) make it a clear defensive play in an uncertain macro backdrop," said McDermott.

McDermott ranks No. 726 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.80%. See Exxon Mobil Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Moving on to natural gas producer Expand Energy. The company announced a quarterly base dividend of $0.575 per share to be paid on Sept. 3. At an annualized dividend of $2.30 per share, EXE offers a yield of about 2.3%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reaffirmed a buy rating on Expand Energy stock and increased his price target to $113 from $99. Mehta attributed his higher price target to cash flow improvement and a strong outlook for shareholder returns. He also cited the stock's attractive valuation, with an 11% free cash flow, or FCF, yield based on his 2027/2028 estimates compared with the peer average of 9%.

The five-star analyst expects Expand Energy to generate FCF per share of about $10 in FY28, up from the prior estimate of about $8 at his mid-cycle price forecast of $3.50/MMBtu Henry Hub. Mehta raised his FCF estimate to reflect an improved outlook for share repurchases, operational efficiencies, and gas pricing realizations.

Furthermore, the analyst expects the recently announced $1.25 billion Twin Eagle acquisition to enhance the competitive positioning of the combined business to expand to premium markets and capture incremental deals in the power and LNG end markets.

Mehta ranks No. 449 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.1%. See Expand Energy Insider Trading on TipRanks.

Mehta is also bullish on Diamondback Energy, an independent oil and natural gas company focused on energy reserves that are primarily in the Permian Basin in West Texas. The company recently paid a base cash dividend of $1.10 per share for the second quarter of 2026. FANG stock offers a dividend yield of 2.2%.

Mehta increased his price target on FANG stock to $220 from $212 and reiterated a buy rating, citing the company's capital-efficient volume growth as oil prices remain high amid persistent supply disruption in the Middle East.

"We remain constructive on FANG, a high-quality, pure-play Permian Basin operator that continues to drive incremental capital efficiencies and strong well productivity relative to peers," said Mehta.

Also, the analyst said that the removal of a minimum return of capital commitment gives Diamondback more flexibility in taking capital allocation decisions amid high oil price volatility and macro uncertainty.

Mehta added that Diamondback sees a favorable environment for production growth, driven by the potential need for global inventory restocking. He highlighted that during Q2 2026, FANG's production volumes of 1,018 Mboe/d exceeded the high end of the company's guidance, driven by a stronger-than-expected natural gas output from Barnett development and improved downstream gas marketing.

Overall, Mehta is optimistic about Diamondback's growth and sees more upside in the stock, given higher production estimates, solid productivity and a deepening position in the Barnett shale formation. See Diamondback Energy Financials on TipRanks.

The Warriors were on the wrong side of history Monday night.

Golden State lost 115-94 to the Pelicans and became the first team in the NBA history to win a game by 50-plus points, but then lose their next three games by a combined 50-plus points, according to OptaSTATS.

Yaxel Lendeborg #1 of the Golden State Warriors reacts during the fourth quarter of the NBA game against the New Orleans Pelicans at Smoothie King Center on Nov. 16, 2026 in New Orleans, Louisiana. Getty Images

#2834. Investors look to Nvidia's earnings and Anthropic's IPO to refresh the tech rally

I've spent the past 20 years pushing back against the periodically popular notion that markets are replaying the late-1990s and early-2000s tech bubble and bust.

We've simply never matched the levels of gross overvaluation, pervasive public greed, fevered IPO issuance, corporate malinvestment and Wall Street malfeasance that set up a generational market peak, a halving of the S&P 500 over two-and-a-half years and a lost decade for stocks.

We still haven't. Even if June was the peak in AI speculative fervor, as seems possible, and if Oct. 6 proves to have been a consequential broad-market top, the major indexes simply never stretched far enough into the thin air to make a potential retrenchment nearly as damaging as the early 2000s were.

Still, in a more muted fashion, markets have been behaving in ways that generate uncomfortable comparisons to past buying climaxes accompanying a technology revolution.

There is still room and time for the market to prove that the messy sell-down in AI-reliant growth stocks is simply a painful adjustment phase and the S&P 500's six-month purgatorial range a mid-cycle refresh. But it will need to start getting on with it.

Everyone wants to celebrate the way the market has absorbed the damage to semiconductors and other AI-buildout plays, rendering it a controlled demolition rather than indiscriminate destruction.

The Financial Times Unhedged newsletter on Monday called it "The perfect sell-off," the same day the Wall Street Journal cheered, "When Nvidia Stumbled, Equal-Weight Funds Delivered."

This is true, as far as it goes. A rush into defensive and "quality" stocks has largely offset the drag from Nvidia's $2 trillion market-cap loss from its October peak.

But recent defensive leadership, especially from low-growth consumer staples and Big Pharma, sends a less-reassuring macro message, and those groups lack the earnings momentum, narrative appeal or market-cap heft to carry a bull market to new heights.

BTIG technical strategist Jonathan Krinsky has noted echoes of the 2000 period when "boring" defensive stocks ripped as tech rolled over, supporting the tape for a while. Barclays equity desk noted Monday that the "quality factor" basket of stocks just had one of the best 100-day runs ever of the past 20 years.

And JP Morgan here plots the stampede into "low-volatility" stocks, mirroring the hasty retreat from "high-momentum" areas. There's room here for defensives to get even more popular before tripping multi-year extremes, but is that what an index investor should root for?

As for the ascendance of everyone's favorite, the equal-weighted S&P 500, Ned Davis Research analyst London Stockton this month noted that just over 50% of S&P 500 stocks were outperforming the index itself in 2026. Sounds healthy, right? Only to a point.

He explains: "Levels [of stocks outperforming the index] above 58% have most often occurred in and around years where there has been a large bear market." The current phase follows a period when leadership was extremely narrow, producing a "resemblance of the prior three years (2023-2025) to the two years prior to the last Tech bubble (1998-1999) with a very small percentage of companies outperforming the overall S&P 500.

"It is not likely that we are in a situation like in 2000," Stockton concludes, "but this highlights the importance that these large tech stocks at the very least do not decline substantially for the S&P 500 to continue rallying higher."

There are other straws in the wind that harken to prior tech busts:  The Federal Reserve is trying to restrain the corporate sector from overheating in the latter part of a cycle, same as in 1999. Retail traders have maniacally bought the cascading decline in Nvidia shares the past week or so, invoking the voracious dip-buying made famous in the late-'90s which worked until it failed spectacularly.  The market's refusal to pay up for sizzling earnings growth raises alarms about whether they are pulled forward, unsustainable or overstated; profits from 2000 were later revised down radically.

And Nvidia's alleged breach of chip-export rules and the forced departure of CEO Jensen Huang are a reminder that major corporate scandals were a key feature of the Tech Bubble's prolonged reckoning, though nothing resembling the multiple accounting frauds of that era are implied here.

Again, this is the kind of litany of worry and woe that gets recited each time a tech-propelled bull market breaks stride for a bit; more often than not a recovery follows to clear the gloom.

Perhaps Nvidia's results on Tuesday clearing the way for the company to restart stock buybacks and the final allocation of Anthropic's IPO shares will refresh the tech bid. With luck, the huge "hyperscaler" tech-platform stocks will move back to the fore as well-funded stewards of a more sober AI expansion.

Maybe, after weeks of Wall Street insisting that hedge-fund tech-stock positioning is scraping multi-year lows, this will finally presage an upside pain trade.

The vaunted seasonal, post-election tailwinds that the consensus was expecting to fill market's sails and lift the indexes from the floor of an enervating six-month trading range are running late. They need to arrive quite soon. Otherwise, the soggy tape - along with signs of ebbing liquidity reflected in Bitcoin weakness a strong dollar and jumpier equity volatility - will make those tech-bust echoes louder.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Despite pervasive bearish sentiment and the average stock trading near new highs, our historical studies suggest the market remains vulnerable to disappointment. With defensive leadership still dominating, risk appetite weak, and financial conditions still tight, maintaining a short-term cautious/bearish stance still seems warranted."

-Twenty years ago this week, I wrote a cover story for Barron's that antagonized plenty of friends in the stock-trading profession by foretelling a future of fast, cheap electronic markets that has largely come to pass.

Called "Death of the Floor," the piece detailed the regulatory, technological and behavioral shifts that were ushering in high-frequency, low-touch trading and pressuring the old exchange model.

Reading it now, the conclusions seem utterly obvious and inevitable, though at the time there were still 2,500 NYSE floor brokers and specialists trying to remain in the middle of most trades.

"For the specialists who manage trading in individual stocks and for the floor brokers who depend on the in-person auction model, a tough challenge is becoming even tougher. To stay in the game, they must master cybertrading. For the small investor, this is almost entirely good news. Trading costs should continue to fall, while the likelihood of getting the best price should improve."

Happily, exchanges have adapted and evolved – to zero commissions, thousands of ETFs and microsecond executions - and we have a market that can handle multiples of the capital flow that was routine back then.

If I have one regret about the piece, it's that my good friend and former colleague Bob Pisani thought the CNBC reporter skeleton illustrating the article was meant to depict him.

I assured him, many times, that it was not.

-Almost every business relies heavily on its most loyal repeat customers. But this skew seems extreme for online-betting companies.

A Bloomberg piece picked up here by Yahoo Finance reports that one-third of all sports bettors produces some 97% of revenue at digital sportsbooks. It says the 1% most active gamblers on average lost 78 cents of each dollar bet, twice the loss rate of the norm among all customers.

One can view this as simply paying for entertainment (I "lose" 100 cents of every dollar I place on a movie ticket), but the social disapproval toward the industry seems to have grown.

Add it to the swirl of issues surrounding the legal and de-facto decriminalized wagering industry.

Perceived hostility of Congressional Democrats to prediction-markets venues taking sports bets in states that haven't explicitly approved sports gambling is seen as a plus for DraftKings and Flutter.

Meantime, Robinhood has blurred the lines between investing and gambling by aggressively expanding into prediction markets. And on Monday it was reported that Kalshi has looked to combine investing and betting from the opposite direction, acquiring a brokerage firm in order to offer stock trading to its prediction-markets clientele.

Adults should be free to behave unwisely with their money in search of a windfall. But brokers promoting the negative-sum game of wagering while professing to help the little guy accumulate long-term wealth sends a dissonant message.

The ducks that were quacking loudly ahead of SpaceX's June IPO are much quieter in advance of Anthropic's planned debut this week.

Here is the year-to-date performance of two ETFs that hold shares of recent new listings, Renaissance IPO and First Trust US Equity Opportunities, relative to the S&P 500.

As of June 12, when SpaceX priced, these funds had gone vertical as the public worked itself into an anticipatory lather, the Renaissance fund running ahead of the S&P by some 20 percentage points in the prior six months.

Since then, the IPO funds have deflated (both now have large positions in SpaceX, of course) and there's a bit more focus on whether Anthropic's target valuation is justified given the vast rethink of the AI-proliferation thesis.

All else being equal, a more favorable setup now versus then for a market being asked to swallow a new $1 trillion-plus ticker.

#2829. Jake LaRavia is the glue to the Lakers’ starting lineup

With Walker Kessler catching his breath on the bench, Devin Booker saw his opportunity.

Saucers for eyes, Booker made a dash to the rim. He caught the pass and went up for what he thought would be his easiest shot of the game.

Trailing him like his own shadow, however, was Jake LaRavia. The forward swatted the layup attempt away and then found Luka Dončić leaking out ahead for a bucket.

On the Suns' next possession, Jalen Green decided to try his luck against the Lakers' small lineup. The result was the same.

Green's drive and shot at the rim were stonewalled by the 6'8" LaRavia who acted as the team's backline protector. On the other end, the Lakers got a dunk.

These were ultimately just two possessions in another Lakers' win. But they also encapsulate why LaRavia has been an invaluable cog to the team's impressive 12-2 start.

After being inserted into the starting lineup earlier in the season due to an injury to Ziaire Williams, LaRavia's play and fit next to the Lakers' stars since has made it difficult to take him out.

While he doesn't have the springiness or shooting diversity that Williams does, LaRavia makes his impact felt by filling in the gaps.

This is because, unlike the other four players in the Lakers' starting lineup, LaRavia isn't elite or even really good at a singular thing. Instead, he helps everything click into place by being solid in multiple areas.

First and foremost, LaRavia plays hard. It's cliché, but there's immense and often overlooked value in having a player who approaches every game with care.

Sometimes his motor can run too hot, and his aggressiveness leads to whistles. LaRavia has been called for 45 fouls this season — fifth-most among all players.

But in the aggregate, his tendency to enthusiastically do all the "little things" that lead to winning is best illustrated by LaRavia's fingerprints being found all over the Lakers' leaderboards.

He currently is second on the team in deflections, second in offensive rebounds, third in blocks and fourth in total rebounds and assists. These stats also display how LaRavia functionally greases the wheels on both ends for the Lakers.

Sturdier and more stout than Williams, LaRavia has helped the Lakers' starters defensively (86th and 90th percentile in block and steal rates) and on the glass.

The team is performing better but is still poor at preventing second chances with LaRavia on, but has compensated for its weakness on one end by making it a strength on the other end. With LaRavia on, the Lakers' starters have an absurd offensive rebound percentage of 37.9% compared to just 18.2% when replaced with Williams.

LaRavia isn't the sole reason for their recent dominance on the offensive glass, but he is a big part of it.

Through his excellent timing on his crashes and knack for playing physical, LaRavia has gobbled up 8.8% of the Lakers' misses this season. That ranks in the 95th percentile among all forwards.

Beyond his work on the glass, LaRavia has also made major strides in his 3-point shooting.

After a disappointing season where LaRavia struggled from the perimeter and admittedly hit a "mental lapse," he's bounced back in a big way thanks to new shooting mechanics and rekindled confidence.

While not the most aesthetically pleasing windup, it's hard to argue with the results. LaRavia is currently shooting a blistering 53% from the corners and 48% overall from beyond the arc this season.

Those numbers will surely come down with a larger sample, but seeing them go in early, hopefully, will encourage him to keep firing throughout the year.

It's early, but this version of LaRavia on this version of the Lakers has been humming.

The starting five with LaRavia in it is outscoring their opponents by 28.3 points per 100 possessions — the second-best lineup in the NBA — and is holding teams to a defensive rating of just 93.2.

For context, not only would that be the best defense in the league, but it would be 12 points better than the current league leader.

LaRavia's game is an acquired taste. We're not talking caviar. It's more like Brussels sprouts. Hearty, versatile and kinda weird sometimes.

He'll frustratingly still pass up a shot or make an ill-advised drive. But he'll also be the first on the floor for a loose ball and in the right spot for a rotation.

He's nowhere near a perfect player, but LaRavia may be the perfect fit on this team especially at his price point.

After the Lakers were unable to land a certified starting four to replace the gargantuan hole left by LeBron James over the offseason, they headed into the year hoping to fill the void by committee. And they have so far.

To be clear, LaRavia isn't James. Nor is he even likely the long-term answer to the Lakers' starting forward position.

However, he has provided a blueprint for the type of player who works well next to the Lakers' trio of the future. He and the Lakers should chalk that up as a win. Even if he fouls a little too much.

All stats courtesy of Cleaning the Glass unless otherwise stated. You can follow Alex on Bluesky at @alexregla.bsky.social.

#2826. Nvidia warns chip buyers of legal risks tied to China AI access, sources tell CNBC

Nvidia warned its customers that new legislation on Chinese companies remotely accessing its artificial-intelligence chips could expose it and them to U.S. "legal action," sources told CNBC.

The chip giant is facing increasing scrutiny from U.S. lawmakers over Chinese companies' access to its advanced chips, which U.S. export controls restrict from going to China.

Nvidia messaged chip buyers in recent months to caution that the proposed Remote Access Security Act, or RASA, would give the U.S. government broader authority to control access to cloud services in the same way as physical hardware, potentially exposing both parties to legal risk, according to two people with direct knowledge of the matter. The people asked not to be named while discussing private communications.

It also said that, for now, it would not sell its chips to customers who rent more than 50% of the graphics processing units, or GPUs, to Chinese companies, the people said.

Separately, the Financial Times reported earlier this month that Nvidia verbally told data-center clients to direct at least 51% of their services to non-Chinese customers under future contracts.

Regulators have had limited powers to control companies' access to those chips through cloud services hosted overseas.

RASA passed the House of Representatives in January but has not yet passed the Senate. Remote access to the chips has provoked the ire of President Donald Trump's administration, which has sought to keep the chips out of China to maintain the U.S. lead in the AI race.

CNBC reported on Nov. 5 that Chinese lab Moonshot AI sought access to Nvidia chips through data centers in Europe. The Trump administration previously singled the company out, accusing it in July of accessing the chips through centers in East Asia.

The U.S. Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security, or BIS, announced on Monday an interim final rule designed to prevent Chinese companies' remote access to AI chips.

Nvidia and BIS did not respond to a request for comment.

On Sunday, U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent called for a "dramatic shakeup" at Nvidia after Jensen Huang's shock departure as CEO, amid reports of an extramarital affair with a woman U.S. authorities reportedly suspect of spying for China.

Bessent suggested other Nvidia executives should face scrutiny over the chip giant's compliance with export controls.

"I want to keep coming back to what was the culture at Nvidia, and were they pressing these seemingly illicit sales to goose their revenues and their earnings?" Bessent told Fox News.

In an August securities filing, Nvidia said it sought to "strictly comply with all applicable export control regulators" but did not have "physical control of our products after sale" and relied on its customers' and partners' compliance programs.

Huang has been asked to testify before the Senate Banking Committee on Dec. 3.

#2824. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 stocks for the long haul

Global stock markets have been volatile as investors assess high bond yields and continued tensions between the U.S. and Iran. There are concerns about the impact of uncertainty in the Middle East on oil prices and consequently on inflation.

Investors who prefer to look beyond short-term noise and buy stocks with attractive long-term growth potential can follow the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts. The ratings of these experts are backed by in-depth research of a company's financials and thorough analysis of the factors affecting its business performance.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Semiconductor giant Nvidia (NVDA) reported stellar fiscal second-quarter results and reassured investors about continued strength in AI-driven demand for its chips. Moreover, Nvidia's FY28 revenue growth outlook of 70% was well above the Street's expectations.

Reacting to the Q2 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reiterated a buy rating on NVDA and increased his price target to $300 from $288. "This is our Top Pick in the semis group, with a compelling product cycle, exceptional growth and valuation below peers," said the 5-star analyst.

Nvidia delivered impressive quarterly results despite supply constraints, Moore said, but he believes that the FY28 revenue and gross margin commentary mattered more, with a favorable outlook for both measures.

Moore said Nvidia's 70% revenue growth guidance for FY28 was above his own estimate of 52% and the consensus of about 40%. The top-line growth estimate was a "remarkable figure" given supply issues and Moore said he expects Nvidia to continue to address bottlenecks to deliver rapid growth.

Among other takeaways, the analyst said performance metrics for Vera Rubin chips were strong, with Nvidia emphasizing a 30x higher throughput per megawatt and 35x lower token cost compared with Grace Blackwell Ultra.

Moore ranks No. 159 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 24.60%. See Nvidia Statistics on TipRanks.

Ride-hailing and delivery platform Uber Technologies (UBER) was recommended by BMO Capital analyst Brian Pitz, who discussed the company's opportunity in the autonomous vehicle (AV) market in a recent research note that reiterated a buy rating and price target of $119.

The 5-star analyst highlighted Uber's transition into a diversified marketplace, with investors now focusing on its AV growth prospects. The key question is how much value can Uber generate as AVs become commercialized, Pitz asked.

"We continue to believe that Uber's AV strategy is evolving and will become a significant driver of revenue and profitability," the analyst said.

Importantly, Pitz believes that Uber can establish itself as a preferred mobility platform for AV makers, supported by its growing AV infrastructure capabilities and an expanding partner base. This would enable Uber to win a significant share of value as the AV market is commercialized.

Additionally, Pitz noted Uber's enhanced position in the AV value chain, with the company shifting from serving as a distribution channel for robotaxis to becoming a broader platform for AV mobility. The analyst discussed Uber's efforts to capture growth opportunities in the AV market, including higher capital spending, expansion into new markets and building partnerships beyond Alphabet's Waymo.

Pitz ranks No. 574 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of about 12.20%. See Uber Technologies Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Semiconductor company Marvell Technology (MRVL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the second quarter of FY27. But the company's increased FY28 guidance fell short of investors' high expectations.

In reaction to the Q2 FY27 results, KeyBanc analyst John Vinh reaffirmed a buy rating on Marvell Technology, with a price target of $400. The analyst noted that Marvell's results and outlook were driven by strength in its data center business. The Q2 FY27 data center business grew 46% year-over-year, to $2.17 billion, far above KeyBanc's estimate of $2.09 billion.

Management "highlighted accelerating AI networking demand across scale-out, scale-across and scale-up architectures, supported by strong 800G optical DSP demand, rapid 1.6T ramp, broader 51.2T switch deployments and higher TIA/driver demand," Vinh said.

Consequently, Marvell raised its data center revenue guidance, now expecting 60% growth in FY27 and more than 60% in FY28.

Regarding the recently announced $120 billion warrant arrangement with Google, Vinh said management argued that programs tied to the warrant are already priced into the custom outlook through FY28. Meanwhile, new programs and those in execution are expected to drive significant upside to the previous FY29 custom XPU revenue estimate of over $10 billion, Vinh said, citing management.

#2820. Trump has arrived in Asia. Here’s what he said about Iran and Deutsche Bank

U.S. President Donald Trump has arrived for his high-stakes trip to Asia, and took a range of questions on subjects from Iran to Deutsche Bank leaking details about his tax.

The president landed in Manila, Philippines, for the initial leg of his trip on Tuesday and answered rapid-fire questions from the press while meeting with other leaders.

Asked about a possible Iranian threat to Washington, D.C., he said that, if there were an attack, Iran would "pay a price the likes of which no country has ever paid before."

The question was about CNN reporting that U.S. intelligence suggested the country was planning to target senior members of Trump's administration with a dirty bomb or chemical weapon in the U.S. capital.

"We take it very seriously because they happen to be crazy," Trump said. CNBC has not independently verified CNN's report.

But the president also said he believed his administration was "very close to getting it done" when it came to the Iran war.

While Trump was airborne, the Financial Times reported Deutsche Bank had mistakenly leaked details of his taxes in March 2017, during his first presidential term. The mistake was kept from the White House at the time, according to the FT.

In a statement, Deutsche Bank said the issues detailed in Tuesday's media reports go back many years. "Where relevant, we reviewed these matters at the time and worked closely with regulators," the lender said.

Asked about the situation in Manila, the president said, "I paid a lot of tax."

"They shouldn't have done it. It's illegal for them to do it," he added.

Trump was also asked about Nvidia's announcement on Tuesday of a new National Security and Export Control Oversight Committee. The company, which reports earnings later on Tuesday, is reeling from the sudden departure of CEO Jensen Huang, who resigned last week after having an affair with a woman U.S. intelligence reportedly believes to be a Chinese spy.

"It's a great company. It's a great idea," Trump said.

Trump is due to fly to Shenzhen, China, where he is scheduled to meet Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin for a three-way meeting on Thursday, on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit.

He is also due to hold a separate bilateral meeting with Xi. Asked by a reporter if he would have a separate meeting with only Putin, Trump said: "I don't know, but I would, if it's available, I would do that. He's coming. I mean, it's really with the guests. I am the guest of President Xi."

Trump met the leaders of Australia, India and Japan as part of the Quad grouping in the Philippines and the prime minister of Thailand. He is due to China on Wednesday.

Speaking at a dinner with Southeast Asian leaders as the event wrapped up for the day, Trump touted his record during his second term, citing investment into the U.S., trade deals made with countries represented by attendees at the dinner, energy infrastructure buildouts and America's AI industry.

Trump, who has led a campaign to rename artificial intelligence "super intelligence," or "SI," told leaders around the table that the U.S. is "leading the world" in the technology.

"We have the world's most powerful SI ecosystem," he said. "We're leading everybody by a lot, and we hope to keep it that way, and we will keep it that way."

He added that the U.S. has "the strongest and most capable military on the planet," pointing to investments in the armed forces and the country's defense sector.

"We make the greatest equipment. You saw that in Venezuela. You see it in Iran. You see it everywhere," Trump said.

#2818. Giannis Antetokounmpo on facing Milwaukee: 'It’s going to be just another game'

Is anyone believing this? Including Giannis Antetokounmpo?

Wednesday night, the Miami Heat will host Milwaukee and face the Bucks, the first time that Antetokounmpo will play in another uniform against the team where he spent 13 seasons and won an NBA title. It's a game everyone had circled on the calendar, although the big one is Jan. 28 when Miami plays in Milwaukee.

However, when asked about it after the Heat's impressive win against the Rockets Monday night, Antetokounmpo tried to play it down as just another game.

"It's going to be just another game. I'm going to do the same thing. I'm going to eat the same meal. I'm going to kiss my kids before they go to bed, get ready, wake up, do my routine and get ready for the game. It's the same game. It's the same thing. It doesn't change. It doesn't matter the jersey...

"I haven't found myself being emotional. I think the only time I've been emotional was when we won the championship. I think I played my whole career, I haven't like found myself like putting emotion to the game. I feel like emotions are for movies, and this is just basketball."

He can say that because the game is in Miami, but Bucks fans are not going to feel the same way — this is emotional.

Antetokounmpo is the greatest Bucks player of all time and he put together a Hall of Fame resume while in Milwaukee: NBA champion and Finals MVP in 2021, two-time MVP, Defensive Player of the Year, nine All-NBA teams and 10 All-Star teams. He won the team its second NBA title, the first since the 1970s. Beyond the on-court success, he built a life in Milwaukee and invested in the city, doing charity work and more.

The exit got a little messy because of that investment — while there may have been basketball reasons for Antetokounmpo to ask for a trade earlier, he didn't want to be the bad guy forcing his way out of the only city and away from the only franchise he had known. Eventually, he felt he had to if he was going to win another ring, and he landed in Miami for a package of picks and players that will help the Bucks on the long road to rebuilding.

Antetokounmpo and Miami come to Milwaukee having found a groove and winning five straight, including beating a good Houston team to improve to 8-6 (good enough for the No. 4 seed in the East right now). Milwaukee is headed the opposite direction, having lost 10 straight.

It is going to be emotional on Wednesday, even if Antetokounmpo won't admit it.

#2813. Top Wall Street analysts recommend these 3 dividend stocks for higher returns

Dividend-paying stocks can enhance portfolio returns by providing a steady income stream to investors and the potential for capital appreciation. Several investors seek exposure to dividend stocks in a volatile backdrop to ensure stable income.

In this regard, recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, backed by strong financials and cash flows to support consistent dividend payments.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Energy Transfer (ET) is a diversified midstream energy company with about 140,000 miles of pipelines and associated energy infrastructure in 44 states. The company made a quarterly cash distribution of 34 cents per common unit for the second quarter of 2026. At an annualized payment of $1.36 per common unit, ET offers a yield of 6.3%.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Jeremy Tonet reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer stock and slightly raised his price target to $25 from $24. The analyst noted that the company delivered a strong Q2 beat across all key metrics.

Tonet highlighted that Energy Transfer raised its 2026 adjusted EBITDA guidance to $18.8 billion-$19.1 billion from $18.2 billion-$18.6 billion, reflecting robust base results and cost optimization. The 5-star analyst added that the company "tightened" its capex to $5.6 billion–$5.9 billion and expects capex above $5 billion annually through 2029, given solid opportunities.

About the Hugh Brinson pipeline, Tonet noted Phase 1 (1.5 bcfd) reaching full capacity by September 1 and Phase II capacity (0.7 bcfd) going online by Q1 2027. The analyst also noted completion of a 14-mile Hugh Brinson lateral in Abilene, Texas, and final stages of talks for an additional 250 mmcfd of Oklahoma power plant demand.

"All in, we see ET well positioned to continue capitalizing on organic growth opportunities across the value chain," said Tonet.

Tonet ranks No. 922 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 9%. See Energy Transfer Financials on TipRanks.

This week's second dividend pick is independent oil and natural gas company Permian Resources (PR). The company declared a base dividend of $0.16 per share for the third quarter, payable on September 30, 2026. At an annualized dividend of $0.64 per share, PR offers a yield of about 2.7%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Permian Resources stock and increased his price target to $27 from $22, citing an improved outlook on oil production growth and operational efficiencies.

The 5-star analyst noted that while PR stock has outperformed its peers so far in 2026, he sees further upside. Mehta is confident about the company's operational improvements and ability to deliver incremental free cash flow per share through the strategic ground game program. He added that the program has completed about $1.05 billion in bolt-on deals this year (as of August 5).

"We see the continued success of PR's ground game as constructive toward future inventory replenishment and increasing working interest over time," said Mehta.

The analyst also emphasized Permian Resources' solid track record of generating growth in free cash flow (FCF) per share. Mehta expects the company's free cash flow per share to grow at a 20% CAGR (compound annual growth rate) from 2025–2028.

Mehta ranks No. 401 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.8%. See Permian Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

Moving on to Sempra Energy (SRE), a utility holding company. Earlier this month, the company declared a quarterly dividend of $0.6575 per share, payable on October 15. At an annualized dividend of $2.63 per share, SRE stock offers a yield of about 3.1%.

Recently, Jefferies analyst Julien Dumoulin-Smith upgraded SRE stock to Buy from Hold but slightly lowered the price target to $97 from $101. The 5-star analyst noted that Sempra stock is trading at a price/earnings multiple that is 14% below its electric peers due to concerns about Texas transmission capital expenditure and the failure of California legislation.

Smith noted that the derating of weaker California peers PG&E (PCG) and Edison International (EIX) is weighing on the sum-of-the-parts (SOTP) valuation but is becoming less relevant given their materially higher fire risk compared to Sempra Energy.

Furthermore, Smith expects Sempra Energy's Texas capex plan to stay in place despite the opposition to transmission and data centers, though delays to 765-kV projects are likely. While a full recovery for SRE stock will need a clearer outcome from the Texas legislative session, Smith believes that the stock may already be an early opportunity for patient investors.

"We are early on the upgrade but see limited downside at current levels as the market is pricing in little transmission upside," said Smith.

Smith ranks No. 945 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.2%. See Sempra ETF Exposure on TipRanks.

#2812. U.S. agencies say they initiated investigation into Nvidia partner Megaspeed in 2024

The U.S. Commerce and Justice Department on Monday stateside said they had initiated an investigation into Singapore-based company Megaspeed in 2024 to "address inaccurate information circulating in public reporting."

Megaspeed International, a cloud computing firm that operates data centers across Southeast Asia, has been drawn into the fallout surrounding the leadership shake-up at chip giant Nvidia.

In a joint release, the departments outlined a timeline of their investigations into Megaspeed, saying the probes began in 2024 over potential export-control violations.

In June 2025, Nvidia placed Megaspeed on its "no sell" list, after Commerce Department investigators requested records from Nvidia concerning direct or indirect sales to the company.

In the same month, U.S. officials and Singapore police conducted an in-person inspection of Megaspeed and interviewed Alice Huang, who was allegedly involved in a relationship with Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang.

"Following this enforcement action, the Department of Justice took the lead role in the criminal investigation of Megaspeed," the statement said.

On Wednesday, Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick also reposted the statement, adding, "The Department of Commerce works to protect the U.S. economy and our critical industries every day. We are very proud of the work of the Bureau of Industry and Security."

In response to the statement, Chris McGuire, senior fellow for China and emerging technologies at the Council of Foreign Relations wrote on X, "Very, very strange statement tonight... about Megaspeed. The facts here just don't add up, and this does nothing to explain why Commerce did not cut Megaspeed off from chips."McGuire said that Megaspeed kept buying chips after Nvidia put them on their no-trade list, and asked if Nvidia also blacklisted Megaspeed's subsidaries Speedmatrix and PT Megaspeed International. "I would bet they did not! If they kept shipping to them after blacklisting Megaspeed, that would be very concerning," McGuire added.

The statement comes after Singapore's Ministry of Home Affairs said the first time authorities there learned of U.S. interest in Megaspeed and Alice Huang was in June. Singapore had already been conducting its own independent investigation and said she was not investigated for espionage.

The city-state barred Alice Huang from re-entering the country in February 2026 after authorities found that she had knowingly provided inaccurate information about her role at Megaspeed.

Alice Huang was reportedly recruited by China's Ministry of State Security to gather information on Jensen Huang. The Justice Department has not reached a conclusion on whether she was a Chinese spy.

Jensen Huang resigned as CEO last week after revelations about multiple extramarital relationships, including with two Nvidia employees. The tech giant has appointed Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress as interim CEO.

The agencies' statement also comes as Nvidia prepares to report its third-quarter results Tuesday afternoon U.S. time, with Kress set to lead her first earnings call.

Earlier on Monday in the U.S., the Commerce Department moved to close a loophole that has allowed Chinese companies to access Nvidia's advanced computing power through data centers in third countries.

The interim final rule from the department's Bureau of Industry and Security is aimed at restricting remote access to advanced U.S. computing technology.

It also comes amid growing scrutiny of Lutnick for not having done enough to prevent restricted Nvidia chips and computing capacity from reaching Chinese companies.

— CNBC's Anniek Bao and Dan Mangan contributed to this report.

Former Michigan Wolverines delivered touchdowns, another multi-sack performance and contributions across special teams in Week 10 of the NFL season. But the biggest news was a difficult development for one of the program's most recognizable NFL stars.

Here's how Michigan's former players fared through Sunday night.

Blake Corum turned 17 carries into 100 yards and a touchdown in the Rams' 34-6 victory over the Arizona Cardinals. He averaged 5.9 yards per carry and added three receptions for 19 yards, finishing with 119 total yards. It was his sixth game in a row with a touchdown.

Two other former Wolverines contributed to the victory: Josaiah Stewart recorded one tackle, and Josh Wallace finished with two.

Edge rusher Derrick Moore recorded two sacks for the second consecutive game in Detroit's 35-10 win over New England in Munich, Germany. The rookie now has four sacks across his last two outings, giving the Lions another productive option up front.

Unfortunately, Detroit's victory came with a major loss. Aidan Hutchinson recorded a first-quarter sack before injuring his knee while celebrating. Initial testing revealed a torn ACL that will end his 2026 season.

The injury cuts short another season after his broken leg in 2024. But for Moore, his recent production takes on added significance as Detroit moves forward without its established pass rusher.

Nico Collins caught nine passes for 94 yards and a touchdown in Houston's 27-24 loss to Cleveland. He drew a career-high 16 targets and reached the end zone for the second consecutive game, following his touchdown against the Chargers a week earlier.

#2805. 7 smart reasons to sell a stock—and my ‘golden rule’ for offloading shares, says hedge fund CEO

Nobody is born knowing anything innately about the stock market. And if you feel like you've missed the investing class, you didn't. Usually there isn't one.

So responsible people who are trying to do the responsible thing by building a portfolio for themselves and their families often find huge gaps in their knowledge and don't know where to turn.

As the co-founder and CEO of the hedge fund Metropolitan Capital Advisors and a longtime panelist on CNBC's Fast Money, I know that there's no shortage of advice when you're getting started about great investments to buy. It can feel easy and gratifying, especially when your portfolio is doing well. But then things get tougher.

One of the hardest things to do as an investor is to figure out when to sell. You buy a stock because you believe it will go up in value. But the decision to sell is rarely so straightforward. Personally, I've made just about every selling mistake. But over time, I've developed a framework for knowing when to sell — and when not to.

Selling decisions are often rooted in emotion and cognitive bias rather than logic. One major culprit: anchoring to past performance, a flawed reference point that clouds clear thinking.

Behavioral economics, particularly prospect theory, shows that we experience the pain of a loss more intensely than the joy of a gain. This leads investors to cling to losers ("just need to get back to even") and sell winners too early ("lock in the gain").

We also place too much emphasis on recent events, a phenomenon known as recency bias. If your last tech stock soared, you're more likely to chase another. If your last trade flopped, you may avoid the market altogether. And when recent trades go well, people tend to trade more — often to their detriment.

Finally, selling triggers a storm of second-guessing and rationalization. Here are my responses to common excuses:

These biases and emotional landmines are why you need a clear, disciplined plan for when to sell, including when:

When you've decided to sell for a solid reason — especially the first, second, or third outlined above — don't hesitate. Don't wait for the perfect price. Just sell.

Just as important as knowing when to sell is knowing when not to sell. Avoid emotionally driven decisions that sabotage long-term gains.

Selling well is an advanced investing skill that demands discipline, logic and self-awareness, and there's no place for ego. If you create a sound game plan, you'll be equipped to act confidently, even in the face of uncertainty. I keep a list of reasons to sell handy so I can literally look at it and keep my wits about me.

Karen Finerman is co-founder and CEO of the New York-based hedge fund Metropolitan Capital Advisors and a longtime panelist on CNBC's Fast Money. She is the author of the New York Times bestselling book, "Finerman's Rules: Secrets I'd Only Tell My Daughters About Business and Life" and the host of the podcast How She Does It. Karen is a proud member of the Wharton Board of Advisors, her alma mater, and serves on the board of The Michael J. Fox Foundation for Parkinson's Research.

#2804. Pistons vs Spurs preview: No Duncan or Duren, others must step up

The Detroit Pistons are down four rotation players as they match up with Tobias Harris and the San Antonio Spurs. Harris has not made his season debut due to a nagging calf injury. That hasn't affected the Spurs in the slightest.

The absences of Duncan Robinson, Ron Holland, Isaiah Joe, and Jalen Duren surely make a difference for Detroit. The shooters will be extremely missed, with Victor Wembanyama's presence shrinking the floor.

Duren has some eye-popping moments against the MVP candidate, but we won't see that big man clash until late March, unless they line it up in the NBA cup Finals.

Where: Frost Bank Center, San Antonio, TX

There aren't too many games where Cade Cunningham doesn't step on the floor as the guy. He can be him in any matchup, but it will take a lot to top Wemby. Considering how small Detroit is currently, their hands are full with the 7-foot-4 Frenchman.

John Collins started at the 5 over the last three games. With Robinson out, don't be surprised if Paul Reed re-enters the starting group. That isn't a Goliath pairing, but some teams opt for as much size as possible when defending Wemby.

The "put the small on Wemby" philosophy hasn't been fruitful this year. Wemby is dunking everything. Per Basketball-Reference, 27 percent of his shots are slams. That's way up from 18 percent last year. Detroit could have success "forcing" him to shoot. He's shooting 32 percent on triples and 39 percent on all middies.

That's a tough ask. Detroit's defense came up short against Giannis Antetokounmpo and the Heat Saturday night. Giannis scored 24 points in the paint. That same type of shots-at-the-rim challenge is up tonight.

Here's a glass-half-full takeaway from Collins. He's made at least two 3s in each of the past three games. Yes, glass half full for sure. Plus, he is shooting 38 percent from 3 on the season. Teams haven't cared, though. They still treat him like a maybe shooter. San Antonio will absolutely dare him to shoot from the wings tonight.

Detroit needs the open ones. Collins has to start hitting enough for teams to feel they need to stay attached. The defensive rotations can get better. The rim protection will not get better. That's not who he is. He's been disappointing defensively and on the glass. The shooting has to be feared enough to aid the spacing — high percentages on low volume won't shift defenses.

The Spurs are the second-best defense in the league, only trailing the Minnesota Timberwolves. While Wemby suffocates you, the "smalls" relentlessly hound.

Stephon Castle and Dylan Harper are big guards who get after it. Castle especially is an All-Defense caliber guy. Cade plays to win, but he'll want to let loose against Castle, who gave him trouble in the second February game last year.

Cade was better in the first one and led Detroit to the dub. He'll try to do that again tonight with an undermanned roster.

Ausar Thompson will do some leading too, as he's had the ball in his hands a lot more. According to Databallr, here's his on-ball percentage by year:

Ausar got his first triple-double a few games ago. He was so involved, zipping up and down the floor. Detroit needs all of that and then some tonight. He has to stay on the floor.

In the dawg category, Daniss Jenkins is in the 100th percentile. He steps up whenever Detroit is counted out. No Robinson, so he'll surely start. The Spurs don't allow many 3s, so Jenkins needs to be ready to knock down when he gets his chances.

Jenkins creates a lot of chances with his I don't care who's guarding me mentality, so expect him to get them up, with the paint being limited.

You can't fault his philosophy either. Jenkins is shooting 42 percent on five 3s a game. He makes them count. Detroit will need every ounce of his fearlessness as they travel to San Antonio. The Spurs are winners of 12 in a row. Detroit will rarely be nine-point underdogs. Let's see how they come out in this position.

Detroit Pistons (8-4): Cade Cunningham, Daniss Jenkins, Ausar Thompson, John Collins, Paul Reed

San Antonio Spurs (12-2): De'Aaron Fox, Dylan Harper, Stephon Castle, Devin Vassell, Victor Wembanyama

#2803. Top Wall Street analysts find these 3 stocks attractive as long-term investments

Investors have been grappling with stock market volatility, weighing elevated oil prices, persistent geopolitical tensions, the Federal Reserve's rate hike and a debate about AI safety. For all that, it helps to look beyond short-term noise and focus on stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors gain clarity, as these experts conduct in-depth research to pick stocks with attractive growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Enterprise software and cloud infrastructure company Oracle (ORCL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the first quarter of fiscal 2027. The quarter benefited from solid growth in cloud infrastructure revenue.

After hosting a series of investor meetings with Ken Bond, Oracle head of investor relations following Q1 earnings, Guggenheim analyst John DiFucci reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $400. Oracle remains a Guggenheim Best Idea, he said.

"We continue to believe ORCL is a decade stock, driven by the massive (and profitable) opportunity ahead for AI training and inferencing," said DiFucci. He also cited the traditional Public Cloud business and additional AI-enabled cloud database and cloud applications for his bullish stance. DiFucci expects all the factors to drive revenue and profit acceleration over the next few years.

While Oracle delivered 850 MW of data center capacity in Q1 FY27, Bond cautioned that Infrastructure as a Service revenue in any quarter depends more on the timing of when that capacity comes online, than on the headline MW number. DiFucci expects IaaS growth to stay strong, with full-year growth expected above Q1 FY27's 120% constant currency rate. Management expects total revenue growth to accelerate sequentially throughout fiscal 2027, as the faster-growing infrastructure business makes up a larger share of the mix.

DiFucci addressed concerns regarding customer concentration, saying OpenAI's share of total remaining performance obligations has dropped from 80% last June to about 50% now, and is expected to keep falling as new contracts are signed with new and existing customers.

DiFucci ranks No. 263 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 62% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17.5%. See Oracle Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

Space systems and launch services provider Rocket Lab (RKLB) was recently initiated as a buy with an $80 price target by Raymond James analyst Brian Gesuale.

"RKLB has assembled launch, spacecraft, components, payloads and optical communications, with Neutron and Iridium completing medium-lift and applications/spectrum in 2027," said Gesuale.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that with the completion of the Iridium acquisition, Rocket Lab will become the only vertically integrated space company with positive free cash flow (FCF). The deal will add a deployed constellation valued at more than $3 billion, globally coordinated L-band spectrum and about $500 million in earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) and $300 million in free cash flow (FCF).

Gesuale highlighted RKLB's strong revenue and backlog growth and progress toward positive EBITDA and FCF in 2027/2028. Rocket Lab is moving from a heavy investment phase into monetization, with estimated revenue growth of 59% in 2026, the analyst said, noting that RKLB's backlog has more than doubled to almost $2.4 billion from $1.1 billion in Q3 2025.

Given strong growth projections, the analyst expects RKLB's gross margins to expand from about a 35% trough in 2027 to about 47% by 2030, while R&D spending is estimated to decline from ~35% of sales in 2026 to about 10% in 2028, creating substantial operating leverage as the platform matures.

Gesuale ranks No. 369 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17%. See Rocket Lab Statistics on TipRanks.

J.P. Morgan analyst Doug Anmuth recently upgraded Meta Platforms (META) to buy from hold and raised his price target to $820 from $640. Despite the year-to-date underperformance of META, the 5-star analyst still sees significant upside, as Meta is in the early stages of launching frontier models and AI-powered offerings beyond advertising, particularly the Muse AI agent and Meta Model API access.

"Frontier models are at the core of Meta's product and monetization pipeline over a multi-year period, and its path toward superintelligence," said Anmuth.

In the summer of 2025, Meta set a goal to rebuild its Meta Superintelligence Labs team and deliver frontier AI models within a year, the analyst said. The team appears to have met its goal, rapidly advancing from the rollout of Muse Spark 1.1 in July to Muse Spark 1.3, which is competitive with Claude and GPT models, Anmuth said.

Meanwhile, Meta's Watermelon product will expand opportunities in consumer products, business intelligence, and Family of Apps engagement, the analyst said. The product will enhance internal operations and overall efficiency, said Anmuth, who's confident of Meta's ability to bring consumer-driven AI products to its extensive user base of 4 billion and views its distribution network as a competitive advantage.

While Meta has focused on frontier models and AI product development, Anmuth also sees solid opportunity in its AI infrastructure capacity, which he believes will support future compute needs.

Anmuth ranks No. 832 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of about 9.40%. See Meta Platforms Financials on TipRanks.

#2801. St. Louis Fed's Musalem says four more rate hikes may be needed in coming months

St. Louis Federal Reserve President Alberto Musalem indicated Tuesday that the central bank may need as many as four rate hikes in coming months, offering one of the most hawkish views yet by a Fed policymaker about the steps needed to quell inflation.

During a speech delivered in Argentina, Musalem made the case that current policy is not significantly restricting economic activity.

"The economy will likely require several interest rate increases before the spring of 2027 to achieve 2% inflation in a timely manner," he said.

Musalem said the real policy rate, which subtracts the inflation rate from the Fed's target rate, needs to be between 2%-2.5% by next spring, and estimated that the current rate is about a full point below that range. That position suggests the Fed may need to hike at each of the upcoming Fed meetings in December, January, March and April.

Musalem does not get a vote on the rate-setting Federal Open Market Committee over the period where he says that the rate increases will be needed, though he does get to present views and provide input on the "dot plot" of individual members' expectations. He will next vote in 2028.

To be sure, Musalem noted "an unlikely but still plausible scenario" in which no further hikes would be needed.

"Falling energy prices and lower shipping costs, or an increase in potential output growth driven by higher productivity growth, might ease inflation pressures such that inflation moves toward target in 18 months," he said. Another possibility is that demand might decelerate as spending on artificial intelligence slows.

However, he said the more likely scenario is that "persistent strong demand and ongoing supply pressures" combine to keep inflation above the Fed's 2% target, thus requiring further increases in the fed funds rate from its current range of 3.75%-4%.

"It would be preferable for a higher real policy rate to happen in a balanced way, driven both by lower inflation and a higher nominal policy rate," he said. "However, the nominal policy rate may need to do most of the work."

#2800. NBA Panic Meter: Can Pelicans fix this mess? Do Suns need to make a move?

We're about a month into the season, the perfect time to check in on some underperforming situations around the league. Are these overreactions given the small sample sizes? Or are there deeper things at play that will continue to be magnified? Let's take a look.

Stop me if you've heard this before, but New Orleans is a mess.

Players taking laps around the court in the midst of a near-60-point loss. Cryptic messages in pressers. Star players retweeting reports of a trade request. (I'm not joking, this is the craziest thing I've ever seen since covering this sport.)

ESPN broke news of Dejounte Murray's wishes to depart from the franchise, but the growing consensus among rival executives is that Murray's value has gotten to a point where he cannot be moved unless there is quality draft compensation attached to him, sources told Yahoo Sports. The whispers of growing discontent behind the scenes related to the accelerated hires of head coach Jamahl Mosley (this season) and by proxy, executive vice president of basketball operations Joe Dumars (last season), had only grown louder amid the Pelicans' recent six-game losing streak (they ended the streak Monday, defeating the Steph Curry-less Warriors), sources said, citing confusion as to why extensive searches were not conducted on either front.

(Henry Russell/Yahoo Sports Illustration)

As it pertains to Murray, according to sources, the thinking is simple. His unhappiness and hope for a new environment is not new and has been discussed privately since last season, with a growing sense that his reluctance to return earlier from his January 2025 Achilles tear was in hopes of being moved at last year's deadline. But it was rather difficult to define a market for an injured player (Murray returned later that month). The decision to draft Jeremiah Fears last summer was another clear indicator that New Orleans was open to moving in another direction, which sources said upset Murray — echoing similar sentiments to his departures from San Antonio and Atlanta.

"What a f***ing mess," one high-ranking executive from a rival team said. "No one wants to touch him."

The veteran guard is a polarizing talent when healthy and engaged, but finding the harmony of both has been quite the challenge over his decade-long career.

The Suns opting to go ZeroPG in their starting lineup (word to Matt Harmon) is going about exactly as you might have expected. Phoenix started the season losing five of their first six games and is currently a game under .500. But it hasn't been its 25th-ranked defense that has been the talk of the town. It's the utter lack of cohesion and control at the other end.

A glance at metrics would show you the Suns actually are 11th in overall (and half-court) offensive efficiency, which is juiced somewhat, thanks to some funky transitional opportunities (the Suns are third in points off turnovers because of an aggressive on-ball scheme that generates mistakes). They're just outside the bottom 10, though, in passing rate, assist points created and potential assists.

And they're losing the Jalen Green-at-point-guard minutes badly. Phoenix has been outscored by 137 points in the 346 minutes Green has been on the floor, according to PBP Stats. Quite simply, it hasn't gone to plan. When Green was initially traded as part of the blockbuster move sending Kevin Durant to Houston, sources say one of Green's reasons for optimism was the consistent word from the Suns that he would be given on-ball opportunities — an element of his game that gradually faded with the emergence of Amen Thompson. The numbers, at least in theory, backed it up; Green was a low-turnover, high-usage guy that showed promise in spots as a creator when called upon.

But part of the charm with Green's playmaking was it came within the flow of his aggression, utilizing his speed and power. Factor into some offseason roster moves, notably inserting Miles Bridges (who isn't one to turn down a drive), more reliance on Devin Booker and less on Green, and you have yourself a player being shoehorned into a role that doesn't suit him or the team. Green's drive rate is the lowest it's been in four seasons and he's converting shots and holding on to the ball at career lows. (He's also shooting around 64% from the charity stripe this month and has yet to crack 30% from deep.) And it's led league executives to believe the 24-year-old, with one year left on his deal and a $36 million player option for next year, is attainable — and that Green wouldn't be opposed to a better fit elsewhere. It's unclear at this stage what Green's current market looks like, but New Orleans, which has kept tabs on him since he was in Houston, just might be paying more attention.

I'll admit, this might feel a tad bit nitpicky.

The Heat are riding a five-game winning streak, have won seven of their last nine, and sit just one game off third in the East after a horrendous start to the season. Klay Thompson has effectively doubled his scoring output this month, Miami just dropped 137 on a top-10 defensive unit and the Giannis Antetokounmpo-Bam Adebayo tandem seems to be working out some early kinks.

The Heat are a stone's throw outside the bottom-five in defensive rating, 29th in opponent 3-point and midrange conversion rate, and 29th in half-court defense.

With any metric — especially this early — some noise is expected. We also don't typically have a decent grasp on a team until about 15-20 games. So yes, there will be some bad opponent shooting luck.

But part of Miami's selling point as a contender was the versatility of Antetokounmpo, Adebayo and Davion Mitchell, and some of those concessions meant assuming it was its offense, not defense, that would need time to jell. Given the uneven start, two central questions surrounding Miami's defensive output are: have the schemes changed since Giannis' arrival, and is this working?

For starters, the Heat are throwing out a lot less zone than normal. Last season, nearly 17% of their defensive possessions featured a zone defense, which has dropped over 10%(!), according to Synergy tracking data. (Miami was also allowing a little less than a point per possession on such instances, now sitting closer to league average.) They're switching more than normal but not getting the same results: bottom 10 in points allowed (that's not solely a Giannis/Bam issue, but they're not fully absolved).

Looking more closely, issues remain with that tandem. Opponents are shying away from the rim when both are on the floor (in their most-used lineup), but the ones that dare to attack are converting over 70% of chances. In addition, when they give up 3s, opponents are shooting 50% from deep. Hardly the recipe for success. Antetokounmpo and Adebayo are also contesting less, not getting enough ball pressure and just not looking aggressive in passing lanes (their "Big 3" on defense are hovering around 50% in steals and deflection rates).

I'm not overly worried, but I'm also cooling down on my prediction that this will end up as a top-five defense by season's end.

I understand why everyone (including this committee of one) was so high on Charlotte heading into the year. There was always going to be a risk in trading LaMelo Ball and his cerebral playmaking, undoubtedly the heartbeat of one of the NBA's premier offenses — but the Hornets seemed equipped to handle it. Charles Lee, one of the brightest young minds around, was still in charge. So were Brandon Miller and Kon Knueppel, to whom the keys were to be given, as well as Naz Reid's unicorn floor spacing and Coby White's tantalizing projections as lead ball-handler.

So how does this team find itself five games under .500 and ranked 30th in offense and half-court efficiency?

A few reasons. Knueppel has played just six minutes, re-aggravating the same left hamstring that kept him sidelined during the preseason. White just returned this month after suffering a calf strain and continues to be on a minutes restriction. Miller has shown no confidence in handling the lion's share of touches ceded by Ball and Miles Bridges, averaging the highest usage and lowest shot-making efficiency of his career thus far, making just 28 of 110 3-point attempts.

And the misuse of Reid has been puzzling to say the least. Over his last three seasons in Minnesota, Reid's 3-point volume accounted for around half of his offensive usage. That has been sliced down to less than 40%, which ranks in just the 34th percentile among forwards, with only 7% of his attempts coming from the corners (14th percentile). It's been a work in progress to say the least, which explains why Reid's frustration, combined with Lee's no-nonsense approach, led to a benching earlier in the month.

But this is as much of a problem of Lee's as it is the new pieces in place. The Hornets have become too predictable and stagnant, not enough rim presence and no real control, either. Being one of the best offensive rebounding and worst ball-security teams is like taking two steps forward and four back. Getting Knueppel back will solve quite a bit — as will simplifying Miller's role and expectations — but it doesn't look good in Charlotte right now.

#2799. Houston Dash hire former NWSL Coach of the Year Seb Hines as next head coach

The Houston Dash have hired Seb Hines, formerly of the Orlando Pride, as its next head coach, the club announced Tuesday.

Hines won the 2024 NWSL Shield and Championship with the Pride, firsts for the club, and was also voted Coach of the Year that season. The Pride fired him in late August 2026, and he will be the seventh head coach in Dash history.

"Seb brings a track record of success that truly aligns with our ambitions for the Houston Dash. He understands what it takes to compete for championships in the NWSL, because he has done it before, and we are confident in his ability to maximize the potential of our roster and establish the standards necessary for sustained success," said Dash president of women's soccer Angela Hucles Mangano in a club statement.

"Our ambition is bigger than a single season or postseason appearance; we want to build a team that consistently competes at the highest level and contends for trophies. We believe Seb is the right leader to help us realize that ambition," Hucles added.

The Dash finished 12th in the league despite a strong rookie class and a bright start, winning three of their first four matches of the season. Forward Kat Rader was one goal shy of tying the record for most goals scored by a rookie in a season (10) and earned a Rookie of the Year nomination. Midfielder Linda Ullmark was named to the U.S. roster for the U-20 Women's World Cup, which took place this September in Poland. Veteran Dash goalkeeper Jane Campbell was also nominated for a Goalkeeper of the Year award this season.

In September, the Dash parted ways with former head coach Fabrice Gautrat and technical director Twila Kilgore, who was the interim head coach of the senior U.S. women's national team. Then-assistant coach Jaime Frias was promoted to interim head coach for the remainder of the Dash's season.

Hines will likely hire a new coaching staff in his new role in Houston. The two assistant coaches he worked with in Orlando have since become head coaches. Yolanda Thomas was promoted to interim head coach after Hines' departure and hired permanently last week as head coach of the Pride, becoming the first Black female head coach in NWSL history. Giles Barnes, also a former Pride assistant coach, left the club in July and leads Al Qadsiah FC as head coach in the Saudi Women's Premier League.

"Ultimately, we want to build something this city and the fans can be proud of, give them a team they are excited to watch and create sustained success for this club," Hines said in a statement. "I look forward to working closely with Angela, our ownership group and the players as we turn that shared vision into results and that work is underway."

With Hines and Thomas as managers, the NWSL will have two Black permanent head coaches for the first time in the league.

#2798. Week 11 Power Rankings Round Up: Packers come back down to earth

Well, Green Bay's matchup against Minnesota went about as badly as it could have, as the Packers fell 30-10. As it turns out, relying on Jordan Love to play at superhuman levels has its drawbacks. Love has answered the call at every turn and played admirably, all things considered, in Week 10, but it turns out that football is a team game, and offensive line play is an important part of that team.

As doom and gloom as Sunday was, there were some positives. J. Michael Sturdivant caught his first career touchdown, Kaleb Johnson showed some flashes when he had time to run, and Jordan Love finally drew a roughing the passer penalty, resulting in probably the loudest cheer of the day aside from the pregame flyover.

It really has been a drastic swing of emotions for the green and gold faithful. There has been some really ugly football, some incredibly good football as we saw in New England, and then a swing back to really bad in Week 10. As much as people blame the offensive line, it truly was a team effort, or lack thereof. Green Bay's defense gave up a touchdown on a 3rd and 23, Trey Smack missed another field goal, the line did let up 8 sacks, and the team coughed up 3 fumbles to go with a Jordan Love interception.

In last week's various Power Rankings, many had the Packers inside the Top 10, with Sports Illustrated having the team as high as the 5 spot in its rankings. One week later, Green Bay still remains in the top half of the league, perhaps as a testament to the strength of the Vikings. Or perhaps it is due to the Packers still finding themselves a playoff team, a true reminder that one game isn't the be-all and end-all in the grand scheme of things.

Thankfully, Green Bay will not lose this week, as the bye week is finally here. Every year, it seems like the bye week comes at the perfect time, but this year, that really rings true. It will be the shortest bye week in the NFL, as both the Rams and Packers will come off their respective bye weeks to play on Thanksgiving Eve a week from Wednesday. Josh Jacobs could be back, Micah Parsons should be back, and Kevin Zeitler will have another week to heal up and ramp up. So with the bye week here, let's see where Green Bay stands around the league:

NFL.com's Nick Shook has the Packers as the highest mark on the collective lists this week, keeping Green Bay at the 10th spot, dropping 2 spots from the 8th spot. Shook writes:

"We're now entering the edge of the quagmire. Green Bay's reliance on the explosive play became a weakness against Minnesota, as a lack of sustainable success contributed to its 20-point loss to the division rival. One might simply say the Vikings have the Packers' number; I tend to think Minnesota merely exposed a weakness that might hold back Green Bay as it fights to remain in the playoff hunt in the toughest division in football. That's not enough to knock the Pack out of the top 10, but it does shake my confidence in them."

Suffice to say, Green Bay is still viewed relatively favorably around the league. Despite falling 7 spots in Conor Orr's rankings on Sports Illustrated, the Packers still hold the 12 spot in his metrics. So it certainly could be worse. Week 11 will kick off in a couple of days, and with Green Bay sitting idle on the bye week this week, they do not control whether they rise or fall. Enjoy the week of stress-free football!
