#2748. Titans place WR Wan'Dale Robinson on IR due to ankle injury, sign Xavier Restrepo to active roster

The Tennessee Titans are adding wide receiver Wan'Dale Robinson to injured reserve due to an ankle injury, per team reporter Jim Wyatt.

In a corresponding move, the team is signing wide receiver Xavier Restrepo to the active roster.

Robinson suffered the ankle injury in the team's Week 10 loss to the Jacksonville Jaguars and could not return to the game. Restrepo was activated from the practice squad on game day and finished with 31 yards on three receptions.

Restrepo may be a familiar name to college football fans. He played for the University of Miami through the 2024 season, his final year with Cam Ward at quarterback. He finished that year with 1,127 yards and 11 touchdowns.

Restrepo hasn't held onto an active roster spot until now, but his former rapport with Ward may help the Titans offense get on the same page and into a groove.

Robinson is in his first year with the Titans after signing a 4-year, $70 million deal this offseason. He has been in the mix for the team's top wide receiver along with rookie Carnell Tate and Elic Ayomanor.

Robinson has played in all nine games for Tennessee this season. He has brought in 38 receptions for 272 yards and a team-high three receiving touchdowns.

Head coach Robert Saleh has not revealed the extent of Robinson's ankle injury. If the wideout returns this season, he would first be eligible to be activated off IR in Week 15, ahead of the team's game against the Indianapolis Colts.

Tennessee will be without Robinson for at least the games against the Dallas Cowboys, Jacksonville Jaguars, Washington Commanders and Detroit Lions.

#2747. Instant thoughts after Notre Dame basketball falls to No. 1 Florida

Twenty-one random thoughts from the home office back in Indiana while watching Notre Dame basketball lose its first game this season, 86-78, to top-ranked Florida in the quarterfinals of the Players Festival 8 in Las Vegas ...

∎ A ton of good stuff there in the second half from Notre Dame, be it Brady Koehler (17 points, five rebounds) or Braeden Shrewsberry (20 points, five rebounds) or Logan Imes or Ethan Roberts. There was a time when it looked like this one would get away from Notre Dame, which was back within three with 11:20 left and six with 4:28. Against that team? Take it.

More: A lot more to like in this one for Notre Dame basketball against SC State

∎ The Irish (4-1) have lost their last three games against No. 1 ranked teams since last beating top-ranked (USA Today poll) North Carolina at home on Feb. 6, 2016. Notre Dame has eight victories over No. 1 teams in program history.

∎ Down by 16 in the opening minute of the second half? Just where Notre Dame wanted it. The Irish kept hanging around, kept chipping away, kept getting some stops. Like that, a 20-9 run helped the Irish get back within five with 11:36 left. Put some game pressure on those other guys.

∎ The Irish got back to within three and had a chance to tie, but Shrewsberry couldn't get a 3 to fall. Next time down, Koehler's 3 rattled in and out and halfway down and jumped out. Denzel Aberdeen banged in a 3 at the other end and like that, the deficit was back to nine.

∎ Florida likely wondered at points in the second half, don't those guys ever go away? Other Irish teams? Maybe. This one? Different.

∎ Chalk this one up to not enough made shots, not enough stops and too many second and third chances for the other guys. The Irish can weather too many missed opportunities on one end, but not both.

∎ The last time Notre Dame played a No. 1 team – back in February back at Purcell Pavilion – it trailed No. 1 Duke at half 54-22. On Tuesday, it was 47-32 at the break, so there's that.

∎ Could Notre Dame make a trade for one of Florida's bigs at halftime? Maybe for a guard or a swingman to be named later? Would have liked the Irish chance if that could have happened.

∎ Outrebounded 20-7 in the first half and allowing 22 points in the paint is too much for any team to overcome.

∎ A 17-4 Florida run dumped Notre Dame into its largest deficit of the season – 14 points – with 2:54 left in the first half. Notre Dame never led the first 20 minutes and trailed by as many as 16.

∎ Playing in Las Vegas each of the last three seasons often has seen Notre Dame bring its "A" compete game. The five Irish losses prior to Tuesday, three of which were to ranked teams, were by an average of 7.2 points.

∎ Lost in the loss was the steps taken by guard Jonathan Sanderson, who often looked like a freshman the first four games. He then went for 14 points and two assists in 21 minutes against the Gators and looked/played like he belonged.

∎ Think fifth-year wing guard Ethan Roberts was high on the priority list on Florida's scout? After going for 25 points on nine-of-10 from the field and seven-of-eight from 3 in the first half in last week's win over South Carolina State to earn Atlantic Coast Conference player of the week honors, Roberts was 0-for-2 from 3 for two points in the first 20. Roberts didn't get his first field goal until 14:50 remained. It came on a drive. Roberts labored to 10 points and finished 0-for-3 from 3.

∎ The Irish tumbled into a double-digit deficit (15-5) the first 8:20, but climbed back to get within two, 15-13, thanks to an 8-0 run buoyed by 3-pointers from two bench guys – Logan Imes and Sanderson. When the Irish could get stops and get out in transition, they got some good stuff. Playing against a halfcourt defense? Not so much.

∎ A window of opportunity opened for the Irish just over halfway through the first half when Boogie Fland left to have an ailing right shoulder tended to and Rueben Chinyelu left with his second foul. Florida still had more than enough options to counter a 13-4 Irish run. This one got away from Notre Dame AFTER those two Florida starters departed.

∎ This was a game that Notre Dame really could have used sophomore center Tommy Ahneman for his size, rim protection and option to get something done around the bucket. The 6-foot-10, 255-pound Ahneman is out for six to eight weeks with a right arm (wrist) injury suffered in the Nov. 9 win over Eastern Illinois. Ahneman did not have surgery, but has since had his right wrist area in a cast, where it will remain for about seven weeks.

∎ Why was Ahneman missed? That Florida front line of Thomas Haugh, Rueben Chinleyu and Alex Condon came into Tuesday averaging a combined 41.1 points, 25.6 rebounds and 8.3 assists. The three then went for 39 points, 22 rebounds and nine assists. Florida's first four baskets were all point-blank looks. An early challenge with no Irish answer, really all night.

∎ Notre Dame basketball had started to make a little noise nationally. On Monday, Notre Dame received three votes in the weekly Associated Press (media) national poll – two more votes than ACC colleague North Carolina. Regardless of the game against Florida, Notre Dame has a chance in November and December to make a little more racket – and garner a few more votes – in the Associated Press and USA Today coaches' polls. Notre Dame has not been ranked in the USA Today poll since January 13, 2018, before a 69-68 home loss to North Carolina bumped it from No. 25.

∎ Earlier Tuesday, Notre Dame basketball took to social media to unveil the green uniforms it will wear this year, which begs the question – why green? The color doesn't hold anywhere near the historic significance as it does with football (Green Machine, right Digger?). Notre Dame won't make a uniform splash nationally until it decides to return to the black and white and green unis it debuted in February 2006 in a win at Seton Hall (the "Urban Irish," as former head coach Mike Brey said) and the "mustard" golds it rode in 2015 all the way to coming this close to beating Kentucky in an NCAA regional final. Want a look that pops? Go with those two, starting with the mustard golds. Back in black, too. Green? Meh.

∎ At tip-off Tuesday, the temperature in Las Vegas was 60 degrees and sunny. Back in South Bend, it was 54 degrees and sunny. Vegas sharps would call that a push, no?

∎ Death, taxes and games against Rutgers in Las Vegas in November. For the third straight season and the third straight Players Era, Notre Dame and Rutgers will meet in Vegas, this time, a second-round game Wednesday, Nov. 18 at 2 p.m. In a quarterfinal contest that preceded Notre Dame-Florida, Rutgers (3-1) lost 85-73 to No. 7 Houston, which Notre Dame has also played the previous two years at Players Era.

#2746. Barlow ‘making progress,' Embiid out vs. Pacers, more on Sixers

Barlow 'making progress,' Embiid out vs. Pacers, more on Sixers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Dominick Barlow decided to finish his post-practice workout Saturday with a windmill dunk.

While Barlow remains out with a "lower back stress reaction" listing on the Sixers' injury report, it's one sign that he's trending up in his rehab process.

"He's making progress," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "He was a part of the practice today. It's kind of a game day type of practice … so it wasn't super intense, as you might expect. But he was out there doing some stuff. He's progressing. Just don't know when he'll be ready to go, but at least he's making some progress now."

Barlow was one of the Sixers' top role players last season, starting 59 games and contributing in a variety of areas. He averaged 7.7 points, 4.8 rebounds, 1.2 assists and 0.9 steals in 23.8 minutes per game.

"Size, athleticism, rebounding," Dean Wade said of the fifth-year forward. "He's another guy that can switch one through five."

Whenever the 6-foot-9 Barlow is available for his season debut, the Sixers will welcome him back as another option at center.

With Ariel Hukporti's preseason Achilles tendon tear and Adem Bona's cervical spine sprain, the team is light on healthy resources at the five. And Joel Embiid (right foot plantar fascia injury management) will sit Sunday afternoon vs. the Pacers on the first leg of a back-to-back. He'll presumably return to the lineup when the Sixers visit the Nets on Monday night.

"I think (Barlow) is probably going to have to play more five than we envisioned," Nurse said, "but Jabari (Walker) is doing that as well. If the situation calls for that, I imagine that's where he'll help the most."

With the Delaware Blue Coats hosting the Maine Red Claws on Friday and Saturday, the Sixers saw an opportunity for two players outside of the regular rotation to log minutes with their G League affiliate.

Rookie guard Labaron Philon Jr. suited up Friday and third-year wing Justin Edwards played both games. Philon posted 20 points, nine rebounds and eight assists in a blowout loss. Edwards shot just 1 for 10 from the floor in the first game, but he scored 23 points on 9-for-17 shooting in Saturday's win.

Nurse has largely kept a tight rotation early in the season and the 10-3 Sixers haven't had many lopsided games with ample garbage time. As of Saturday night, the Sixers ranked 29th in the NBA in bench minutes per game.

"My first belief is it's hard to get better unless you're playing," Nurse said. "That kind of trumps everything, in my opinion. Just historically, we've had a number of guys that have gone down to those games, respected it … and it's really helped them in their careers. … It's been a minute for these guys — both of them — since they've had any real court time.

"So that fit into the schedule well. … I think it will also help them to just find some rhythm, find their legs a little bit. We're going to need everybody at some point, so keep 'em going."

VJ Edgecombe was among the crowd Friday night at Chase Fieldhouse.

"They're my brothers, man," he said. "Labaron, Justin, (Caleb Love), (Rayan Rupert). I just wanted to show love. … I didn't have (anything) to do yesterday. … We have that relationship off the court, too. I'm just being supportive and showing them that I care about them."

#2745. Buffalo Bills DB Te’Cory Couch signed to practice squad

The Buffalo Bills announced a series of roster moves as they begin preparations to host the Miami Dolphins at Highmark Stadium for Week 11. Buffalo has signed defensive back Te'Cory Couch to the practice squad, bringing him back to One Bills Drive after being released ahead of last weekend's game and once he cleared waivers Monday afternoon.

The 27-year-old defender out of the University of Miami has played in four games for Buffalo this season. In those games, Couch has made eight tackles (6 solo), showcasing a knack for limiting big plays after the catch with solid tackling. Couch joined the Bills in 2024, entering the NFL as an undrafted rookie free agent.

To make room for Couch, Buffalo once again parted ways with veteran offensive guard Nick Broeker, who has been re-added to the team's practice squad several times already this season. Broeker has yet to appear in a regular-season game for the Bills, though he has previously played in 12 games (2023 and 2024 seasons) for the Denver Broncos. Broeker was originally drafted by Buffalo in Round 7 (230th overall) of the 2023 NFL Draft.

The Bills also announced that rookie offensive guard Ar'maj Reed-Adams has been placed on Injured Reserve, which will sideline him for at least the next four games. With Reed-Adams heading to IR, that opens up a spot on Buffalo's 53-man roster.

A potential candidate to take over that roster opening is veteran wide receiver Terrell Shavers, whose return-to-practice window was recently opened as the next step toward a return to the field following a devastating knee injury against the Jacksonville Jaguars during the Bills' 2025 NFL playoff run.

#2744. MVC NOTEBOOK: As Paradise Jam awaits, Graves assesses ISU's 2-2 start

The cellphone on Matthew Graves' desk in the Indiana State Sycamore basketball coaches office rings.

A visitor awaits nearby. Mark Slessinger, Graves' associate head coach, heads toward the ensuing practice in the adjacent ISU Arena.

It's a busy Tuesday afternoon, especially with the Sycamores preparing to catch a 2:30 a.m. flight — with one stop — to St. Thomas. ISU will play three games in four days at the Paradise Jam, an eight-team tournament at the University of the Virgin Islands, beginning with Friday's 4 p.m. matchup with Stephen F. Austin.

Graves' Sycamores enter the event with a 2-2 record. They lost 78-70 to Charlotte in Terre Haute, won 69-66 at Ball State, lost 82-73 at home to Milwaukee, and then routed overmatched Earlham 109-37 in Hulman Center on Saturday.

In the Missouri Valley Conference nonconference standings, the Sycamores are currently among the bottom four teams, joining Evansville at 2-2, with Northern Iowa 2-3 and Illinois State 1-3 going into Tuesday's games. Murray State tops the league at 5-0, so far, after Tuesday afternoon's 82-66 win over Hofstra at the Savage Brothers Showcase in Mississippi. Five Valley teams are 3-1, including Belmont, Drake (an 83-79 loser to Tarleton State on Monday), Illinois-Chicago and Valparaiso (a 106-35 Monday winner over Calumet College of St. Joseph). Southern Illinois is 2-1.

ISU opens the Virgin Islands tournament against a SFA squad that stands 3-1, with its lone loss coming 81-78 in overtime to Rice on Saturday.

Graves believes the Sycamores also could be 3-1 right now, or even 4-0. The debut setback to an athletic Charlotte team was close throughout, but last week's loss to Milwaukee didn't set well with the third-year ISU head coach.

"You want to try to win every game, obviously. The Milwaukee game is the one you look at that we just did not play up to our standards," Graves said Tuesday. "It wasn't that we [should've] won the game or not — that remains to be seen — but we just didn't play a very good basketball game. In the Charlotte game in the opener and at Ball State, I felt like we competed and obviously won one of them and lost the other."

Against Milwaukee, the Sycamores got outrebounded 43-33 and outshot 51.7% to 42.4% from the field and lost an early lead barely five minutes into the game and never regained it.

ISU emerged from that disappointment to overwhelm Earlham — a NCAA Division III program the Sycamores hadn't played since 1927 — and had 12 Sycamores score points. Standout guard Karmani Gregory sat out that game, resting a leg injury incurred against Ball State.

"The Earlham game served its purpose," Graves said.

He figures Gregory will be OK to play in the Paradise Jam tournament, which will include a second-round game against either Temple or Middle Tennessee at 4 p.m. Saturday (if ISU loses to SFA) or 6:30 p.m. Sunday (if ISU beats SFA). Monday's schedule includes a seventh-place game at 1:30 p.m., fourth-place at 4 p.m., third-place at 6 p.m. and the championship at 9 p.m.

"He's available. I wouldn't say he's 100-percent healthy, but he's healthy enough to play," Graves said. "And barring anything crazy the next couple days, I anticipate him being in the starting lineup on Friday."

Gregory ranks third on the MVC scoring list, averaging 21.3 points per game, right behind Southern Illinois' Jalen Haynes at 25 ppg and Drake's Jon Carroll at 22.3.

Haynes and his SIU teammates could face Indiana State at the Paradise Jam. The Salukis loom in the tournament's other bracket and open the event against Tennessee Martin on Friday. The field also includes East Carolina and Utah Valley.

Other MVC teams will trek to tournaments in warmer climes this weekend. Murray State plays Seton Hall on Friday in the Bahamas, Valpo plays Troy on Saturday at the Pensacola Invitational in Florida and Illinois-Chicago plays Miami (Ohio) on Sunday at the Scout Jacksonville Classic in Florida.

#2743. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2742. Trump’s accounts traded up to $97.5 million in Nvidia as Huang gained White House access

President Donald Trump has had plenty of reason to cheer the rise of Nvidia.

The chipmaker helped propel the stock market that Trump is fond of touting. Its billionaire co-founder, Jensen Huang, became one of the president's favorite businessmen. And by the end of last year, Nvidia had become one of Trump's three largest individual stock holdings, worth between $12.5 million and $58 million, up from between $615,000 and $1.3 million a year earlier.

Since returning to the White House, Trump's investment accounts have bought or sold Nvidia shares at least 103 times — transactions worth a combined $27 million to $97.5 million, according to a new CNBC analysis of his annual and periodic financial disclosures, which were most recently updated through August.

By midday Monday, Trump's Nvidia stake, based on the value he reported holding in August, would be worth $4.5 million to $57.7 million with stock priced around $157. But the filings give broad dollar ranges for holdings rather than the number of shares, leaving his current stake unclear.

Now, Huang's resignation as Nvidia's CEO on Thursday, following revelations about his relationship with a woman suspected of spying for China and reports of affairs with company employees, has put those trades back in the spotlight.

Among them was a sale of $500,001 to $1 million on May 18, the first Monday after Trump returned from a state visit to China with Huang. Five days earlier, before Huang boarded Air Force One during a refueling stop in Alaska, Secretary of State Marco Rubio had briefed Trump on intelligence concerning Huang's relationship with the suspected spy, The New York Times reported.

Trump on Monday denied being briefed. Asked about the reported briefing from Rubio, Trump said, "I haven't heard that," adding that he did not know whether the woman, Alice Huang, was a Chinese spy.

The president's financial accounts also bought smaller amounts of Nvidia shares in the days immediately before and after the May 18 sale, part of a broader trading blitz. Such flurries have defined his second term: Trump disclosed more than 21,000 trades in 2025 alone, compared with roughly 500 during his entire first term, according to CNBC's analysis.

A White House spokesperson told CNBC independent advisors manage Trump's portfolio without his input. Trump Organization spokesperson Kimberly Benza said the arrangement is designed to "avoid even the appearance of a conflict of interest." The disclosures offer no evidence Trump directed the trades or that his advisors had access to classified intelligence.

Trump has long used the stock market as a scorecard for the economy, touting its record highs — sometimes when highs have not actually been reached — despite the turbulence caused by his tariffs and the Iran war.

"Look, we have the highest stock market we've ever had," Trump told ESPN commentator and political talk show host Stephen A. Smith on Thursday when pressed about Republicans' midterm losses.

Nvidia alone accounted for about 13.5% of the S&P 500's 2026 gains through September, according to an analysis by Choate Wealth that looked at how chip stocks have driven the year's performance.

Huang's rise in Trump's Washington has been nearly as striking as Nvidia's on Wall Street. The two have met or appeared together at least 15 times since Trump returned to office, according to a CNBC review of public records and news reports. Huang in June told The Associated Press that Trump even "calls me in the middle of the night."

At Trump's Jan. 20, 2025, inauguration, several Silicon Valley titans occupied prime seats, but Huang wasn't there. The two men had never met.

That changed on Jan. 31, 2025, when Huang visited the White House, capping a bruising week for Nvidia: Chinese AI startup DeepSeek had wiped $593 billion off the company's market value in a single day, according to LSEG data cited by Reuters.

Two days before the meeting, Trump's money managers bought between $1 million and $5 million in Nvidia stock.

For Huang, much of the relationship hinged on access to China. In April 2025, the administration effectively halted sales of Nvidia's H20 chips to China, prompting a $4.5 billion charge, according to an SEC filing. Huang argued that withholding U.S. chips would only accelerate China's development of its own.

The administration eventually relented. After another meeting with Trump in July 2025, Huang announced from Beijing that Nvidia expected to resume H20 sales. A week after Trump confirmed an August 2025 deal calling for 15% of the revenue from the company's chip sales to China to go to the U.S. government, his accounts also bought between $5 million and $25 million in Nvidia stock, one of several large tech investments that day.

Huang was also becoming a fixture of Trump's international diplomacy, joining him in Saudi Arabia and the U.K. Nvidia, already a $1 million donor to Trump's inaugural fund, would pledge another $25 million to his planned White House ballroom on condition of anonymity, The Washington Post first reported.

But behind the scenes, intelligence officials first briefed Trump last fall on Huang's relationship with the woman suspected of spying for China, The New York Times reported.

Trump nevertheless continued to ease restrictions on Nvidia's business in China, announcing Dec. 8 that the company could sell its more powerful H200 chips. His accounts had sold as much as $1 million in Nvidia stock four days earlier and bought more the day after.

In the first eight months of 2026, Trump's accounts made another 38 Nvidia trades worth a combined $9.4 million to $28.3 million, according to CNBC's analysis. A disclosure released Oct. 8 — the day Trump awarded Huang the National Medal of Science — showed purchases of up to $1.6 million and sales of up to $1 million in August alone.

Trump stood by Huang as Nvidia's board weighed his future last week, urging him to fight to keep his job, the Times reported.

With Huang out and Nvidia's shares tumbling, Trump described him Thursday as a "tremendous talent" who was "going through hell" — in the same interview in which he boasted of the market's record highs.

Trump is already floating a comeback for Huang, telling reporters Monday that reinstating him "certainly isn't a bad idea." But Nvidia's board is not considering his return, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC. The person asked not to be named because they were discussing internal matters."

#2741. NFL Week 11 betting: 5 biggest storylines, including favorites dominating and some huge point spreads

The Los Angeles Chargers are on the board.

The Chargers weren't just the last NFL team to win a game this season; they were the last team that had not covered the spread. They checked off both boxes on Monday night by beating the Ravens as 4.5-point underdogs. The Chargers and the Philadelphia Eagles are the only teams that have covered the spread just once this season.

Here are the biggest betting storylines for Week 11 of the NFL season:

The NFL season became chalky all of a sudden.

For the second straight week, favorites dominated. In Week 9 favorites were 14-1 straight up and 11-3-1 against the spread. Then in Week 10 it almost repeated, with favorites going 10-3 straight up and 9-4 against the spread going into Monday night's game. The Chargers scored one for the underdogs on Monday night with a win over the Ravens.

Patrick Mahomes and the Kansas City Chiefs were one of the favorites that dominated in Week 10.

Part of the reason for the favorites suddenly dominating is there are more than a few teams that are helplessly out of the playoff race already. We saw that at the trade deadline. The contenders got stronger, with many first- and second-round picks being moved for players. That also meant the also-rans got worse.

Oddsmakers will adjust spreads. We can see that a bit in some huge Week 11 spreads. But we're likely to see the gap between the best and worst teams widen. We'll see if the point spreads keep up with that trend.

Anyone still alive in a survival pool will have multiple options to choose from in Week 11.

There are five double-digit favorites in Week 11. There are the two biggest spreads of the NFL season included in that group. The Chiefs are 16-point favorites over the Cardinals, who are quarterbacked by rookie Carson Beck. The Bills are 16.5-point favorites over the Dolphins, who are quarterbacked by Kyle McCord.

It's projected to be a lopsided week in the NFL, though that's often when we see chaos.

With another big game on Sunday night, Bengals quarterback Joe Burrow took over as the clear favorite in the NFL MVP race.

Burrow is -115 to win MVP, with Josh Allen now trailing at +350. The other big change is that Jordan Love went from the top five of the odds to +6000, making it a four-man race, according to oddsmakers.

Burrow has been on fire and his next two games are against the Commanders and Saints, who don't have great defenses.

We get a fantastic matchup for Sunday night in Week 11. It's a showdown between a 49ers team leading the NFL in offensive EPA (expected points added) and the Vikings, who lead the NFL in defensive EPA. Oddsmakers prefer the 49ers, who are at home. They're 3-point favorites, and even -3.5 at some sportsbooks.

The 49ers have had a remarkable season, and the Vikings looked rejuvenated with Kirk Cousins taking over at quarterback in Week 10. It could be a playoff preview on Sunday night.

The Lions lost defensive end Aidan Hutchinson to a torn ACL on Sunday. They were already without linebacker Jack Campbell. Even though the Lions are without their two best defensive players, the betting market doesn't think there will be a big drop.

The Lions are 6.5-point favorites in Week 11 over the Tampa Bay Buccaneers, who are coming off a bye. And they are strong favorites to still make the playoffs in a crowded NFC wild-card race. The odds for the Lions to make the playoffs at BetMGM are -525. The odds on them missing the playoffs are +400. Apparently the betting market believes the Lions can continue to outscore everyone.

#2739. Always one of a kind, Oubre reflects on Sixers stint, why he chose Pacers

Always one of a kind, Oubre reflects on Sixers stint, why he chose Pacers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Kelly Oubre Jr. experience switched sides Sunday afternoon at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Back in Philadelphia with the visiting Pacers, Oubre was his usual self. There's never any risk of mistaking Oubre for somebody else.

"Love him," Pacers head coach Rick Carlisle said pregame. "Such an upbeat guy. He's such a unique person. Love the wardrobe, all the different stuff. He's a very authentically one-of-a-kind type guy."

Carlise then detailed Oubre's various basketball qualities, some of which he showed in Indiana's 131-124 loss. The 30-year-old lefty wing scored 16 points and grabbed four rebounds. His day also included a theatrical imitation of Jaylen Brown's off-arm push after the Sixers' star was whistled for an offensive foul.

Sixers fans grew familiiar with Oubre's strengths, weaknesses and eccentricities during his three seasons as a Sixer. He wound up becoming the team's third-longest tenured player behind only Joel Embiid and Tyrese Maxey. Oubre played 178 regular-season games and averaged 14.9 points, 5.4 rebounds, 1.6 assists and 1.3 steals.

After the Sixers replaced president of basketball operations Daryl Morey with Mike Gansey, Oubre signed a two-year contract with the Pacers in free agency.

"Mike came over from Cleveland and I heard whispers that he wanted some of his Cleveland guys," Oubre said before the game in the visitors' locker room. "And I was like, 'OK, cool. I'm a Philly guy. So I guess when he comes over, I will no longer be a Philly guy.' That was kind of the situation at hand.

"No bad blood because business is business at the end of the day. They have a great team over there, and they put together a team that they think is the best for them. I'll be all right elsewhere. I figured it out."

Oubre thought Indiana made sense as his next stop.

"It was the pace, the culture, the attitude that this team has," he said. "It's a fast-paced style of basketball. It's a bunch of guys who work collectively as a unit and kind of figure it out from there.

"I thought that would be a great opportunity for me to come and and kind of free-flow play, not have to think. I was wrong. Been thinking a lot, but I'm starting to figure it out and starting to get my rhythm here. … It's a great culture. Coach Rick Carlisle is a legend, Hall of Fame coach. And they wanted me. I love people who want me."

As Oubre settled into a new city, the Sixers had a stunning, transformational summer.

Gansey traded for Jaylen Brown, signed LeBron James, and added veteran role players like (former Cavalier) Dean Wade, Anfernee Simons and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope. Oubre followed along.

"It was must-see TV, like (for) everybody else," he said. "As a basketball fan, it was pretty cool to see where I was kind of reinvent and get new life and new energy. So I was happy with that. Would I have loved to be a part of it? Of course.

"But at the end of the day, I'm a grown man and I've got to keep it moving and figure it out for myself. It was entertaining. … I'm kind of happy for all the people that have sacrificed over these years. … I'm trying to put a road block in that road for y'all."

Oubre joined the Sixers on a minimum-salary deal in September of 2023 after averaging 20.3 points the past season with the Hornets. Head coach Nick Nurse pitched Oubre on his potential to be a valuable, multi-dimensional player for good teams.

"I was literally about to be done with basketball," he said. "Coach Nurse called me multiple times and said, 'I want you to play for me.' And I was like, 'You know what? This guy really seems to care. He really wants me.' So thank you for just pulling me out of that pit and that hole that I was in four years ago. I got some new life in my basketball career.

"I'm always thankful for that and I love Nurse for that. Just appreciative for my time here. The battle wounds and scars that I've been able to build up, it made me a better man."

Throughout his Sixers stint, Oubre often spoke of growth, evolution, adaptation and self-discovery.

He talked again Sunday about his desire to "Be water" and how life as a Sixer changed him.

"I grew a lot, man," Oubre said. "Just the mental fortitude of being able to withstand adversity. Being able to wake up and not necessarily know which soliders are going to battle, just having to put on different hats on any given night. At first it was challenging, because I'm thinking about all the logistics and things and like, 'This is not what they asked me to do.' But at the end of the day, I just bought into being somebody that can be like water and grow with the team.

"It's been a blessing to be able to play with (VJ Edgecombe), be able to watch Tyrese (Maxey) ascend. Those are the guys where we were front line a lot. Just going through war with those guys was amazing. It just taught me mental fortitude. If I can make it through this, I can make it through anything."

Oubre felt he developed a meaningful connection with the Philadelphia fan base over his three years.

"When they talk about the City of Brotherly Love, I think they're talking about your soul, not necessarily about what you can offer them and what you do," he said. "Everybody in this city works hard. They wake up, go to work, keep the city going. And us as the prima donna basketball players, the least we can do is go hard and sacrifice for them so that they have something to cheer about when they come home from work.

"I think that was the relationship that we had. I'm forever indebted and forever grateful to the people in the city of Philadelphia."

#2738. Anthropic likely to price IPO above the current range of $70 to $78, sources tell CNBC

Anthropic is likely to price its IPO above the current range of $70 to $78, multiple sources told CNBC's David Faber on Tuesday.

The artificial intelligence company is planning to close the order books and lock in total demand at 4 p.m. ET on Tuesday, according to the people, who asked not to be named because they were not authorized to speak publicly. Anthropic is slated to list on the Nasdaq under the ticker symbol "ANT" and start trading on Nov. 19.

If Anthropic's IPO proceeds as planned, the company will debut roughly five months after SpaceX went public in a record offering that raised $85.7 billion. But Anthropic is facing a public market that's grown increasingly skeptical of the AI trade, as well as mounting and widespread antipathy toward the technology in the U.S.

A group of researchers, including Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei, co-founded the company in 2021 after defecting from OpenAI, now its chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and it underwent a period of explosive growth in 2026, driven largely by the popularity of its AI coding tool, Claude Code.

For the nine months ended Sept. 30, Anthropic said it generated $33.24 billion in revenue, up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year, according to its prospectus. It reported a $104 billion net loss during the nine-month period, though most of that was due to the change in fair value of convertible notes and derivative liabilities. Anthropic lost $25.1 billion over the same stretch in 2025, the filing said.

Anthropic has long worked to establish a reputation as a safety-conscious AI company, and its prospectus included more than 90 pages of risk factors for investors to consider ahead of its listing.

"I think if we do this right, we can create an age of miracles where humanity is able to solve problems that have eluded us for millennia," Amodei said in Anthropic's roadshow video. "We have to make sure that all the risks, all the ways that things can go wrong with this technology, we very carefully address those risks as we bring the benefits and as we have a viable business at the same time."

"I invite you to join us on the next phase of this journey," Amodei said.

#2735. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2734. Homebuilder sentiment plunges to 14-year low, leading more builders to drop prices

Sentiment among U.S. homebuilders dropped to the lowest level in 14 years, as higher mortgage rates, higher costs for energy, materials and labor, as well as weaker consumer confidence, led to more builders cutting prices.

Builder confidence in the single-family housing market fell four points to 25, according to the National Association of Home Builders/Wells Fargo Housing Market Index, or HMI. That is the lowest since April 2012, when the market was just recovering from the housing crash and foreclosure crisis. Last November, the index stood at 38.

Mortgage rates pulled back slightly in October, but surged higher again last week. The average rate on the 30-year fixed hit 7.51% on Monday, according to Mortgage News Daily. Last year at this time it was 6.38%.

Of the HMI's three components, the index measuring current sales conditions fell six points to 28, and future sales expectations dropped two points to 30. Buyer traffic declined two points to 16 — its lowest level in 15 years, excluding the start of the pandemic in 2020.

"Builders are confronting multiple challenges in today's economy," Bill Owens, NAHB chairman and a home builder and remodeler from Worthington, Ohio, said in a release. "Stepped-up immigration enforcement is keeping legal workers off job sites, deepening labor shortages and driving up costs. Higher fuel prices are prompting subcontractors to raise their rates sharply, and with fewer buyers in the market, more builders are scaling back construction."

More builders are now cutting prices as they try to get buyers in the door. In November, 46% of them reduced prices, the highest share in the post-Covid period, according to the report. The share of builders using sales incentives also hit a post-Covid high of 68%. That was the 20th consecutive month in which at least 60% of builders used them.

"The housing market faces a range of supply-side challenges, including building material costs pushed higher by tariffs, an ongoing shortage of construction workers and excessive regulations that undermine housing affordability," Robert Dietz, NAHB's chief economist, said in the release. "Mortgage rates above 7%, softer consumer confidence and an uncertain economic outlook are holding buyers back."

In the three-month moving averages for regional HMI scores, the Northeast dropped four points to 34, the Midwest fell five points to 35, the South fell three points to 27, and the West posted a three-point decline to 23.

#2733. Observations after Sixers shake off slow start, Embiid injury scare to win

Observations after Sixers shake off slow start, Embiid injury scare to win originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers brushed off their blowout loss to the Celtics and got back in the win column Thursday night.

They overcame a first-quarter deficit as high as 17 points and earned a 123-112 home win over the Raptors. The Sixers moved to 10-3 and Toronto fell to 5-7.

Tyrese Maxey had 27 points and eight assists. Joel Embiid added 20 points.

Jaylen Brown tallied 18 points and six rebounds. VJ Edgecombe posted 15 points, eight rebounds and a season-high eight assists. Anfernee Simons scored 17 points and shot 5 for 6 from three-point range.

RJ Barrett recorded 31 points and 14 rebounds. Immanuel Quickley had 26 points and six assists.

The Sixers were without LeBron James (low back/sciatica management), Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain), Dominick Barlow (lower back stress reaction) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear).

The team told reporters Thursday morning that Bona will be re-evaluated in approximately one week and that Barlow participated in shootaround. The 23-year-old forward also went through a pregame workout on the Xfinity Mobile Arena floor. There's no formal timetable for his return.

Toronto's sidelined players included Kawhi Leonard and Scottie Barnes.

The Sixers will face the Pacers on Sunday afternoon in Philadelphia. Here are observations on their victory over the Raptors:

Dean Wade began the game in James' normal spot. The Sixers started slowly, missing their first foul field goals and opening 1 for 8 from three-point range.

He stayed down after setting a ball screen and having an apparent knee-to-knee collision with Ja'Kobe Walter. The Sixers called timeout with 8:30 left in the first quarter and Embiid grabbed at his right knee. He eventually rose with assistance from teammates and Sixers head athletic trainer Kevin Johnson, then limped back to the locker room. Jabari Walker entered.

At the 5:26 mark of the first quarter, Embiid checked back in to cheers from the home fans.

"I knew that he didn't twist it or anything," Maxey said. "I knew it was a knee-to-knee, so I wasn't too worried. He kept saying, 'I just got kneed.' That still hurts, though."

Embiid ended up logging 28 minutes, though he certainly wanted to play more.

"He was very, very motivated to play," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "It was hard to get him out for his scheduled rests tonight, which is a good problem sometimes."

While Embiid's return was a major positive development, the Sixers fell further behind. The Raptors' lead swelled to 32-16 with a Jamison Battle three-pointer and Nurse burned a timeout.

Simons sunk a buzzer-beating three to conclude the first quarter, cutting the Sixers' deficit to nine points.

"Those are big momentum plays that usually have some weight to them and carry you into the next moment," Nurse said.

The Sixers kept rolling against a shorthanded Toronto team playing on the second night of a back-to-back. Simons nailed a triple to kick off the scoring in the second quarter. Brown buried a mid-range jumper and Edgecombe jammed in a crowd-pleasing dunk to give the Sixers a 35-34 edge.

Brown and Edgecombe shined in the second quarter both as individuals and as a duo. The Sixers need Edgecombe to attack a bit more offensively when he shares the floor with Brown and bench players. It doesn't hurt for the second-year guard to have that clarity about his role in those units.

"He's one of our better playmakers," Maxey said of Edgecombe. "He's really good at getting downhill and creating for himself and others."

The Sixers' only three bench rotation players were Walker, Simons and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope (11 points, five assists). In addition to Walker, Wade got center minutes behind Embiid.

Embiid picked up his third foul with 2:23 left in the second quarter and had to take a seat. The teams were tied at 60 apiece through two periods.

The game remained even for much of the third, but Maxey fueled an 11-0 Sixers run late in the quarter.

His speed and the Sixers' collective focus on pushing the pace whenever possible allowed the team to get regular points before Toronto could set its defense. Maxey zoomed in for a layup, then tossed the ball ahead to Brown for an easy bucket. Brown assisted a Simons three. Walker converted a fast-break lay-in and canned a three on the first possession of the fourth quarter. All of a sudden, the Sixers held a 101-87 lead.

Brown, Edgecombe and the bench gave the Sixers more good minutes early in the fourth quarter. They sealed the deal without any late drama. And the fans chanting for a Tacko Fall appearance got their wish with a final-minute entrance by the 7-foot-6 center.

#2732. Joey Loperfido removed from Houston Astros' 40-man roster, sent to Triple-A Sugar Land

The Houston Astros sent outfielder Joey Loperfido outright to Triple-A Sugar Land on Tuesday, the team announced, removing him from their 40-man roster.

Loperfido was Houston's opening day left fielder in 2026 but played in just 40 games due to injuries and demotions to the minors. He totaled a .214/.311/.330 slash line in 121 major-league plate appearances this season and spent most of the second half at Triple-A.

ALSO SEE: Astros add pitcher Jackson Nezuh to 40-man roster

Last month, Loperfido had arthroscopic surgery on his right hip to address "a labral injury and hip impingement that were identified shortly after the season ended," the Astros said. The team said Loperfido's recovery would delay his availability for the start of spring training next year.

The Astros did not specify a timeline for Loperfido's recovery. Tuesday's transaction removed him from their 40-man roster, which is at 39 players. More moves could occur by Friday, the deadline for clubs to extend contract offers to players who are eligible for salary arbitration.

Loperfido, 27, owns a career .678 OPS in 162 major-league games. Drafted by the Astros, he was traded to the Toronto Blue Jays at the 2024 deadline and re-acquired last offseason in a deal for Jesús Sánchez. He remains in the Astros' system now as outfield depth.

Houston has a clear need to address its outfield this offseason. Astros outfielders combined to rank 29th of 30 major-league clubs in OPS in 2026, with Houston starting 12 different players in left field and eight different players in center.

#2731. Eagles Film Review: What to make of Makai Lemon so far

I wanted to use the bye week to get a better idea of what the Eagles have in Makai Lemon. So I went back through every target from the first nine games, hoping to see how his rookie season was developing. By the end, I was probably more frustrated with the offense than I was when I started. Evaluating a first-round receiver when almost everything thrown his way is a hitch, a quick out, or an underneath crosser is incredibly difficult. At times, this felt like an Eagles' offense disaster compilation, with Lemon unfortunately involved.

His production has improved. After 18 catches for 127 yards through six games, he caught 19 passes for 185 yards and a touchdown over the next three. However, the bigger question for me is how much more he can offer. The Eagles have given us a pretty clear picture of the role they want him to play right now. They have given us far less evidence of what he could become. All statistics in this article are courtesy of Fantasy Points Data Suite.

I liked Lemon as a prospect because he looked like a good football player with a fairly high floor. I still see that. I also questioned his explosiveness and whether he could become a really good receiver. Halfway through his rookie season, too many of those questions remain unanswered because his usage has been so boring.

If you want to see the video with analysis of all targets, it's available on my Patreon here! Have a little spoiler of my favorite route of his…

Lemon's best trait as a route runner is how quickly he can change direction and sell his routes using his whole body. A receiver can have quick feet, but good defensive backs aren't simply staring at his feet. The shoulders and torso have to help sell the route. Lemon has several examples where a subtle body fake gets the defender leaning before he plants and breaks away. His movement in tight spaces looks very natural. He can threaten one direction and get into another without making the whole process look labored.

The fourth-down slant against the Cowboys might be my favorite example of his season. He hesitates briefly on the release before selling the vertical stem, getting the cornerback into a position where he has to respect the possibility of Lemon continuing downfield. Then Lemon breaks inside. The separation comes from making the defender react to a believable threat before changing direction. It is a really clean win.

I particularly liked that play when comparing it with an earlier route against Travis Hunter vs. the Jaguars. On that rep, Lemon takes a hard outside step, but he doesn't close enough to make Hunter feel threatened vertically. Against the Cowboys, Lemon does a much better job of making the release and stem look like the beginning of a vertical route. The opponents are different, so it isn't a perfect comparison, but his improved route-selling is encouraging.

The Giants game provides another lovely example of route detail. Lemon angles his stem slightly, suggesting he might continue inside, then works upfield and gets the defensive back to turn. Once the defender's eyes move away, Lemon uses that blind spot to create separation as he breaks back toward the football. He adds a little physical contact to help himself, then gets his feet organized and catches away from his body. That is proper receiver play.

Lemon understands how to find space against zone. Settling between defenders sounds straightforward until you watch receivers who keep running themselves into coverage. There is a difference between occupying an open patch of grass and adjusting a route as the defenders move. Lemon already shows some of that understanding.

One of my favorite examples comes against the Jaguars. He initially threatens to cross the field, recognizes the linebacker dropping into the space around him, and adjusts back away from that defender. Simply stopping would make him easier to close on. Continuing across would take him toward coverage. Lemon finds a way to remain available, and Jalen Hurts delivers the ball where he can extend and catch it. He creates a usable throwing window, then gives the quarterback room to place the ball slightly outside his frame.

Another example later in the target reel shows him working toward an out route but recognizing he is already free. He doesn't need to keep racing toward the sideline and potentially bring himself closer to the outside defender. He can throttle down, turn, and present a target. Those little decisions are valuable, especially in an offense that has made completing passes look painfully difficult at times.

He is averaging 1.20 yards per route run against zone compared with 0.49 against man. At this stage, I feel more confident in his ability to recognize and use space than in his ability to consistently defeat every type of individual matchup.

Watching all the targets together made this strength really obvious. Lemon repeatedly extends his hands away from his body and catches the football cleanly. He can reach behind himself, get down to a low throw, or turn and secure the ball while moving.

There is a catch vs. the Titans that is a good example. The ball arrives behind him, and he has to adjust before taking contact. Against the Panthers, he sells an inside route before breaking out against a defender with outside leverage. The coverage makes that a difficult route to win, and the throw doesn't make his life especially easy either. He still finishes the catch.

The two drops against the Giants need a mention, and I will get to them. But I came away from this exercise feeling positive about his hands overall. There are too many catches away from his frame, including through contact, for me to suddenly decide that catching the football is a weakness because of the final game before the bye.

Lemon takes some absolute shots in this target reel. He keeps making himself available over the middle anyway. I don't see a receiver whose routes become hesitant because he knows a safety or linebacker is coming downhill. The Cowboys game, in particular, asks a lot of him physically, and he keeps competing through it.

What I like is that his toughness includes some awareness of how to protect himself. His touchdown vs. the Cowboys is a good example. Lemon knows the hit is coming. He secures the ball and turns his body so that he can absorb contact without charging straight into it. A smaller receiver needs to make those adjustments. Being willing to take a hit is valuable; understanding how to finish the catch while reducing unnecessary punishment is even more useful over a season.

He also shows situational awareness. On one fourth-down conversion, he gets the separation, catches the ball, and gets down with the first down secured. There is no need to fight for an extra yard and risk the possession. Elsewhere, he turns upfield and lowers his shoulder when there are yards available.

His blocking against the Rams and Cowboys also stood out this season. He is willing to contribute when the ball goes elsewhere.

A limited role that makes the ceiling difficult to judge

This is the biggest issue with the evaluation, and a lot of it belongs to the offense. Hitches account for 29.5% of Lemon's charted routes, putting him in the 98th percentile among qualified receivers for hitch usage. Nearly three out of every ten routes! His average depth of target is 6.69 yards. He is running routes on 86% of the Eagles' dropbacks, so this isn't simply a rookie waiting for playing time. He is out there regularly, operating within a role that gives us a very narrow view of his ability.

There is nothing wrong with running a hitch. Lemon can catch one, absorb contact, and keep the offense moving. But so much of this film involves turning back toward the quarterback against zone, catching the ball, and getting hit immediately. Some quick outs come on second-and-20 or third-and-13, where the defender can concede the completion and tackle underneath. Those plays provide limited evidence of how Lemon can stress a defense. You expect an NFL receiver to complete some of those assignments.

The slot usage needs some nuance, too. He runs about 65% of his charted routes from the slot, so calling him exclusively a slot receiver would be inaccurate. In these condensed formations, even the meaning of an outside alignment can become a little blurry. However, if he needs to remain inside to be effective and his receiving role stays confined to short routes, that limits the offense's flexibility. It also makes me question the value of using a first-round pick on that particular role. A useful underneath receiver can have a good career. I want the Eagles to find out whether Lemon can give them more than that.

The target reel included one (!) vertical route. One! Obviously, I only watched his targets for this, and vertical routes that aren't targeted still matter, and watching targets alone doesn't tell us how often he was open elsewhere. But a clear-out assignment also doesn't give us the same evidence as a play designed to put the ball in his hands downfield. I want to see how he handles those opportunities when the quarterback is actually looking for him. His college film included slot fades, posts, and corners. Give him some of those chances in the NFL. He might fail. At least we would learn something. They aren't even trying.

I am asking for more vertical opportunities, but I have no idea if he will suddenly become an excellent deep receiver. His short-area quickness is clear. Whether he has another gear once he gets downfield is much less certain. The movement that helps him win a quick slant doesn't automatically translate into running away from an NFL cornerback downfield.

The downfield opportunity against the Commanders captures both the promise and the concern. I love the release. His feet are quick, he uses his upper body to work through the contact, and he gets on top of the cornerback. That ability to stack the defender puts him in position to compete for the pass. After that, though, I don't see an obvious extra burst that allows him to pull away. He appears to be near full speed already, with the defender still close enough to contest.

He also looks back for the ball early. Continuing to run before revealing its arrival makes life harder for the cornerback. Here, Lemon's eyes give the defender a cue to locate it. That is a technical detail worth developing, particularly for a player who may need to maximize small advantages rather than rely on speed to create a huge gap.

Even in college, he wasn't always separating comfortably on vertical routes. He made some difficult catches anyway. I think he could retain some of that contested-catch value despite his size, because he competes for the football and can adjust outside his frame. But the NFL evidence is too limited to make a strong claim. I need more than one shot to decide how much of that game translates.

Lemon is averaging 2.89 yards after the catch per reception, and I don't currently see this as the area that will transform his value. He can avoid contact in tight spaces and bounce off a tackle.

However, he doesn't look like a receiver who will routinely make multiple defenders miss or accelerate away after a simple throw. The early attempts to manufacture touches around the line of scrimmage didn't seem especially well suited to him. Thankfully, those screen targets became less prominent as the season progressed. I would rather get him moving into space on an in-breaking route than keep asking him to create something from a stationary reception with defenders closing.

The design plays a role here, too. Catching a hitch with someone on your back gives you less chance to create than catching in stride with room ahead. I wouldn't treat the YAC figure as a pure measure of his individual ability. Still, the film hasn't shown enough open-field explosiveness for me to assume better usage will turn him into a major YAC threat.

The Giants drops were bad, especially the one on the final drive. That was poor. I just don't think the right conclusion is that Lemon has poor hands. The wider target reel shows too much evidence of good catching technique. Joe Milton's velocity is also part of the adjustment receivers face, although that doesn't excuse the drops against the Giants.

I still like Lemon as a football player. I think he has a pretty high floor. His footwork is good, he understands space against zone, and he repeatedly catches passes outside his frame while taking contact. There is enough here to believe he can become a useful, dependable part of the offense moving forward.

My concern is how much of the first-round investment the Eagles are actually exploring. Some of that is unfair to Lemon. A player doesn't choose where he is drafted, and a productive slot receiver has value. But draft position affects the expectations, and watching this many short targets makes me want considerably more from the role. You cannot spend a first-round pick on a receiver and use him in such a limited way.

Maybe the Eagles see limitations in practice that explain their approach. Maybe the protection and quarterback situation make expanding the passing game difficult. I don't know. I do know that this season has reached the point where evaluating young players should matter enormously. I want to see Lemon targeted on a slot fade, given chances on posts and corners, and asked to develop the vertical technique that currently needs work. He doesn't have to win every rep for those opportunities to be worthwhile.

Right now, I have a much clearer opinion of his floor than his ceiling. The Eagles have shown us that he can contribute in a limited role. In the second half, they need to give him some vertical opportunities for us to see what he can become.

#2730. How LeBron James dissected Pacers defense down the stretch

How LeBron James dissected Pacers defense down the stretch originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

As he overtook Jason Kidd for the third-most assists in NBA history, LeBron James piloted the Sixers' offense down the stretch against the Indiana Pacers on Sunday.

With Joel Embiid sitting out and Tyrese Maxey getting a breather on the bench, James took over in the fourth quarter. He did so by spamming the same action over and over again.

The set consisted of James getting a ball screen from a guard – either VJ Edgecombe or Anfernee Simons – at the top of the key. After setting or ghosting the screen, the guard would short roll towards the basket.

When the Pacers blitzed or hedged the screen, James found the guard. The Sixers could then attack a 4-on-3 or 3-on-2 situation.

Twice, Edgecombe and Simons scored off the roll.

When the Pacers helped to the roller, that opened up the shooters in each corner.

James directed traffic on offense to ensure that the Sixers spaced the floor properly. Here, he waved Dean Wade out of the dunker spot and into the corner. Edgecombe promptly hit him for an open three off the short roll.

After all of the Sixers' success, the Pacers eventually switched the ball screen. That left the 6-foot-4, 173-pound Quenton Jackson on James, who has five inches and 77 pounds on him.

Jackson and James had already gone head to head on Sunday.

In the first quarter, James set a ball screen for Edgecombe which the Pacers switched. Edgecombe fed James in the post and the Pacers doubled him to support Jackson. James punished them for it.

They didn't double James after the switch in the fourth quarter. This time, James backed Jackson down, backed him down again and backed him down some more before scoring inside.

James' strength abusing matchups against smaller defenders after switches has been a safety valve for the Sixers' offense. Guards struggle to stop him from getting to the basket. When defenses send help, he picks them apart with his otherworldly passing ability.

That combination forced the Pacers to pick their poison. From the 9:55 mark in the fourth until the Pacers switched to a zone defense (after James bullied Jackson) with just over 6:00 left, the Sixers scored 13 points out of this action across seven possessions. That's a stellar 1.86 points per possession.

James, who's scoring a career-low 15.1 points per game so far this season, might not be the elite bucket-getter he was for most of his career. But he's still brilliant at conducting highly efficient offense that pressures a defense's pain points.

#2729. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2728. Japan exports in October surge 20.5%, beating estimates on robust chip shipments

Japan's exports in October surged 20.5% from a year earlier, snapping a two-month slowdown in shipments, official data released Wednesday showed.

Exports growth beat expectations of economists polled by Reuters that had pegged it at 20%, and it was sharply higher compared to a revised 16.5% in September.

Shipments of semiconductor equipment that have been propping up Tokyo's exports jumped 49.7% by value. Exports of motor vehicles climbed 19.9% year on year.

In terms of destinations, shipments to China rose 13.3%, while they climbed 23.9% to the U.S.

Exports have been the main driver of Japan's growth, although a surge in the value of imports of petroleum due to the Iran war has offset some the impact of the exports surge on its economy.

Import growth in Japan for October came in at 21.6%, sharply missing analysts' estimates of 25.8%, with petroleum imports climbing 60.5% by value, even as volumes slipped 0.7%.

Analysts previously told CNBC that the surge in value was because Japan was attempting to switch sources to more expensive American oil as shipments from the Middle East stalled.

Imports of petroleum from the U.S. jumped more than 1,100% year on year by value and 738% by volume. In contrast, shipments from the Middle East dipped 16.5% by volume, while higher prices pushed up import value by 37.7%.

The country posted a surprise contraction in its third quarter GDP, which shrank 0.1% on an annualized basis.

Following the data, Japan's Nikkei 225 was up 0.91%, while the yen traded at 159.50 to the dollar.
