#2727. ROUNDUP: With 40-2 record, Pomeroys steam into NAIA volleyball tourney

Saint Mary-of-the-Woods College will make its first NAIA women's volleyball national tournament appearance Saturday when the Pomeroys travel to Cornerstone University for an opening-round match at Mol Arena in Grand Rapids, Mich.

The Pomeroys enter the national tournament with a 40-2 record, extending a season that has produced a school record for victories, the program's first River States Conference regular-season championship and its first NAIA tournament berth.

SMWC completed conference play undefeated and hosted the RSC tournament semifinals and championship match for the first time. In a rare twist, The Woods got nipped 3-2 by IU-Kokomo in the title game Saturday.

Woods coach Ashley Harris earned her first RSC Coach of the Year award, while junior libero Gaby Fernandez was named the conference's Defender of the Year. Fernandez also received five RSC Defender of the Week awards and one NAIA National Defender of the Week honor this season.

SMWC led the conference with eight All-RSC selections, including four first-team honorees. Fernandez, Karla Melendez Martinez, Masa Pesic and Sofia Soto earned first-team recognition. Ariana Cruz-Gonzalez, Luci Morris and Lily Richards were named to the second team, while Leishmarie Virella received third-team honors. Raimey Owens represented the Pomeroys on the RSC Champions of Character Team.

The Pomeroys opened the season with 24 consecutive victories and swept both regular-season meetings with IU Kokomo, including a straight-set home victory over the then-No. 22 Cougars on Sept. 15. SMWC later earned a four-set road victory at IU Kokomo on Oct. 27.

SMWC advanced through the conference tournament with straight-set victories over Brescia and IU Columbus before falling to No. 23 IU Kokomo in five sets in Saturday's championship match. The Pomeroys rallied from a two-set deficit before dropping the final, 25-18, 25-13, 25-27, 20-25, 15-5.

Melendez Martinez finished the championship match with 22 kills and 19 digs, while Fernandez collected 28 digs and nine assists. Virella added 19 digs, Cruz-Gonzalez recorded 25 assists and 11 digs, and Richards contributed 19 assists and 13 digs.

• RSC honors South grad Baker — Saint Mary-of-the-Woods College redshirt sophomore Zayvion Baker has been named the River States Conference Men's Basketball Defensive Player of the Week after recording six steals, five defensive rebounds and one blocked shot in two road games.

The Terre Haute South High School grad collected three steals and one defensive rebound in an 82-69 loss at Bethel (Ind.) on Nov. 10. He added five points in 30 minutes.

Baker followed with three steals, four defensive rebounds and a blocked shot in the Pomeroys' 80-71 victory at Mount Vernon Nazarene (Ohio) on Friday. The 5-foot-11 guard also scored nine points on three 3-pointers and contributed three assists without a turnover.

Baker averaged seven points, three steals and 2.5 defensive rebounds across the two games. He started both contests and averaged 25.5 minutes.

SMWC took a 4-4 record into Tuesday's 5 p.m. home game vs. IU-East, the Pomeroys' RSC opener.

• MVFC cites Wuest's special-teams play — Indiana State wide receiver B.J. Wuest was named the Missouri Valley Football Special Teams Player of the Week on Monday.

The Danville, Ind., native turned in one of the longest plays in Indiana State history Saturday afternoon as the wide receiver lined up in the back of the end zone on Murray State's 53-yard field-goal attempt on the final play of the first half.

The attempt bounced short of the end zone and into Wuest's hands just in front of the back end-zone line. Wuest weaved his way through the Murray State field-goal unit and returned it down the left sideline for the 100-yard touchdown return to cut the score down to 21-14. It marked Wuest's second career special-teams touchdown after blocking a punt and returning it for a score last year at North Dakota.

ISU (2-9 overall, 0-7 MVFC) finishes its season with a 3 p.m. game at first-place South Dakota State (9-2, 6-1) in Dana J. Dykhouse Stadium at Brookings.

• Pomeroys ride well — At New Castle, Saint Mary-of-the-Woods' Western equestrian team earned two high-point team titles and a reserve high-point team finish during three shows Nov. 14-15 at the Henry County Fairgrounds.

The Pomeroys opened the weekend with reserve high-point team honors in the first Purdue-hosted show before claiming high-point team honors in the second. SMWC added another high-point team title in the University of Illinois-hosted show.

Claire Coryell led the Pomeroys individually, earning one high point rider award and two reserve high point rider awards across the three shows. Coryell also qualified for postseason competition in reining alongside teammate Isabella Monnett.

Three additional Pomeroys secured postseason berths. Clair Schoettmer qualified in open horsemanship, Courtney Hamrick in ranch riding and Bella Woodard in rookie horsemanship.

• ISU signs prep standouts — Coach Windy Thees' Indiana State program signed three more high school recruits this week, including Amissville, Va., pitcher Brooke Farmer; South Lake, Texas, outfielder Cate Stoklas; and catcher-utility player Emelia Rousse of West Bloomfield Hills, Mich.

Those signings follow that of Terre Haute North standout Kenzie Zigler.

Farmer, a standout pitcher and two-way player Fauquier High School, earned multiple All-District, All-Region, and All-State honors. As a junior, she recorded 236 strikeouts across 131 innings while posting a 1.443 earned-run average and holding opposing hitters to a .148 batting average. Offensively, she batted .389 with three home runs, 21 RBIs, and three stolen bases.

Stoklas hails from Carroll Senior High School, where she posted a .308 career batting average. Rousse starred at Detroit Country Day Upper School, posting a .555 batting average and a .622 on-base percentage while serving as a key contributor behind the plate and across the field.

#2725. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2724. Observations after Embiid posts season-high 37 points in Sixers' win over Nets

Observations after Embiid posts season-high 37 points in Sixers' win over Nets originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Joel Embiid's season-best 37 points led the Sixers to victory on Monday night in Brooklyn.

The Sixers notched a 135-123 win over the Nets to move to 12-3 on the season. Brooklyn fell to 3-10.

Jaylen Brown tied his season high with 31 points and set a new season high with 13 rebounds. Tyrese Maxey had 24 points and nine assists.

Julius Randle's 33 points topped the Nets.

On the second night of a back-to-back, the Sixers were without LeBron James (lower back/sciatica management), Dominick Barlow (lower back stress reaction), Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear). The Sixers are now 4-0 without James.

They'll visit the Magic on Wednesday night. Here are observations on the Sixers' win against the Nets:

Embiid returned after missing Sunday's victory against the Pacers with the "right foot plantar fascia injury management" injury designation that's become the norm for him early this season.

He had an especially aggressive start in New York and set plenty of middle ball screens for Maxey. When the Nets blitzed or hard hedged Maxey, Embiid found a nice soft spot around the foul line and wasn't hesitant to fire open jumpers. The 32-year-old big man posted 13 points in the first quarter on 6-for-11 shooting (1 for 2 from three-point range).

Beyond the stats, it must be encouraging for the Sixers to watch Embiid trust his instincts and jumper. We'll see whether he can sustain a good run and fully snap out of his shooting slump.

Maxey broke the scoring ice when he swiped a steal and then coasted in for a dunk. Dean Wade's put-back dunk gave the Sixers a 4-0 lead. Wade started in James' place for the fourth time and had eight points and 11 rebounds over 32 minutes.

With James out and Embiid back, Jabari Walker played at both power forward and center. He logged 16 minutes, a number that would've been higher without his foul trouble. Walker scored 12 points and grabbed five rebounds, the latest in a string of strong performances.

The Sixers extended their lead at the start of the second quarter.

Anfernee Simons drilled a corner three. The Sixers' three-man bench shot 6 for 9 from the floor in the first half.

Everyone who played for the Sixers chipped in something positive. Wade leapt up to snag a Walker miss and threw down an and-one dunk. VJ Edgecombe hit a floater to grow the Sixers' advantage to 50-30. Embiid had a well-worn path to the foul line in the second quarter and made 9 of his 10 free throws.

Sixers head coach Nick Nurse kept an eight-man rotation, only playing Simons, Walker and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope off the bench until the final 18.5 seconds.

The Sixers' collective effort on the glass was a major strength in the first half.

All of the starters besides Maxey recorded at least one offensive rebound. Brown had a double-double before halftime and poured in 19 points over the first two quarters.

The Sixers' focus and intensity slipped in the third quarter.

Nolan Traore's layup cut Brooklyn's deficit to single digits. Michael Porter Jr. hit a long two over Caldwell-Pope to trim the Sixers' lead to six points.

The Sixers concluded the third period on a high note. Walker sunk a buzzer-beating three to lift his team to a 105-95 edge. Again, the 24-year-old provided much more than intangible energy and made meaningful, momentum-shifting plays.

The action stopped with 9:02 left in the fourth quarter after Brown was fouled by Randle. The Sixers' star forward appeared to have his forehead collide with Randle's face. He was down for several minutes before going to the bench.

Randle needed stitches in his mouth before re-entering with 5:01 to go, Amy Fadool reported on the NBC Sports Philadelphia broadcast. Brown subbed back in with 6:02 remaining and a bandage on the cut above his left eye. However, he exited at the 1:44 mark and did not return.

Nurse told reporters in Brooklyn that Brown was out at the end of the game because of "tightness," according to PhillyVoice's Adam Aaronson.

After their shaky third quarter, the Sixers were ultimately fine in the fourth and reached a dozen wins.

Egor Demin's jumper pulled the Nets to within 125-121, but Embiid then iced the game by swishing a corner three.

#2723. Wall Street's fear gauge is surging again and this time it's not just about AI jitters

Faced with a barrage of headlines from nationwide tech outages to Nvidia earnings and Anthropic's initial public offering, traders on Tuesday piled into protective put options on the S&P 500 Index at a rate that suggests fear beyond just an AI-specific correction.The Cboe Volatility Index (VIX) surged nearly 5 points to a high of 27.7 at the worst of Tuesday's selling and the ratio of put to call volumes on the SPDR S&P 500 ETF (SPY) hit 1.5, the highest since the first week of April, according to Barchart data. The 'VIX' was last still hovering above 26.With the VIX climbing four days in a row, the volatility gauge hasn't traded under 16 since Oct. 16, arguably trending higher as the S&P 500 slips below this month's lows, trading under 7,300 for the lowest price for the benchmark equity gauge since June. Cboe's three-month volatility gauge, VIX3M, briefly touched the highest since April.

While Nvidia earnings are contributing to the uncertainty – the stock's options currently imply more than a 7% move after its report, the biggest in almost two years – volatility in the S&P 500 jumped more than in the Nasdaq 100 Tuesday, pointing to a more widespread fear among investors."SPX vol is expanding faster than NDX vol, as SPX vol is coming from a lower base," Eric Metz, CIO, SpiderRock Advisors for BlackRock, said via email. "Today, the market is pricing acute near-term stress in large-caps broadly, but tech-specific fear is still notable.Even within the technology cohort, Tuesday's laggards took on a different tone. Software stocks declined the most ahead of Anthropic's IPO, with options volume on the iShares Expanded Tech-Software Sector ETF (IGV) surging to almost four times the 30-day average, according to Cboe LiveVol data. Salesforce was hardest hit, with put contracts outpacing calls on twice the average options volume."There's a lot of reasons to be risk-off," said Noel Smith, co-CIO of Kai Asset Management. "This is a market-wide phenomenon today that's not just AI stocks."To be sure, bulls weren't totally absent from the session, with the Nasdaq 100 Index holding its November low and VIX still under its Nov. 5 high of 28.4."While vol is now at a relatively elevated level, we have not seen much fear in the options market," said VolSignals' Matthew Nadel, citing fixed-strike volatility in S&P 500 options, which measures volatility changes across specific options strikes. "We have seen a pretty decent move higher in SPX fixed strike vol, however the move up in vol has been mostly orderly."

Asa Montgomery, #5 of the United States of America (USA) in action during the FIBA U17 Basketball World Cup - Turkiye 2026 Group A match between the United States of America (USA) and France at Basketball Development Centre in Istanbul, Turkey on June 27, 2026. Jon Scheyer and the Duke Blue Devils are absolutely crushing it on the recruiting trail.

Their latest win comes with a victory over specifically involved foes North Carolina and UConn, both of whom were also going after Matija Lukic.

But on Tuesday, Lukic chose to commit to Duke, according to Rivals.

It continues to build up a stacked Duke Class of 2027, and it seems the Blue Devils can't be stopped from getting the players they want.

Lukic is a 6-foot-9 forward out of Serbia.

Rivals calls him "one of the top international prospects in the 2027 class."

As long as Duke keeps winning like this in recruiting, the Blue Devils should keep racking up lots of wins on the court, too.

#2720. ‘Hard to win': Simons, Walker carry Portland lessons into Sixers roles

'Hard to win': Simons, Walker carry Portland lessons into Sixers roles originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Anfernee Simons and Jabari Walker shared the floor for 128 games in Portland. The Trail Blazers won 43 of them.

They sat beside one another in the Sixers' interview room Thursday night after a victory and discussed what the other 85 taught them.

"Whether you've got a big role or a small role, it's hard to win in the NBA," Simons said. "We've seen it firsthand."

Their roles were considerably different then.

Simons was an established starter entrusted with creating and scoring. Walker, a 2022 second-round pick, was trying to establish himself in a rotation. During their three overlapping seasons, Portland went 33-49, 21-61 and 36-46. The Blazers missed the playoffs each year.

Both are now reserves on a Sixers team constructed to contend. In nine games they've both appeared in this season, Philadelphia is 7-2.

They provided 26 points, 11 rebounds and six three-pointers in the Sixers' 123-112 win over the Raptors at Xfinity Mobile Arena. Simons scored 17 points and shot 5 for 6 from three-point range. Walker recorded nine points and eight rebounds, including four on the offensive glass.

The Sixers outscored Toronto by 24 points during Simons' 23 minutes. Neither former Blazer committed a turnover.

Their production helped Philadelphia overcome a 17-point first-quarter deficit and improve to 10-3 without LeBron James.

"You learn once you come off the bench to kind of read the game before you get in there," Simons said. "Obviously, we started off kind of slow. You just want to go in, bring some energy, get some stops, get out and run. I think everybody who got off the bench brought energy."

Simons supplied an immediate lift with a deep three-pointer at the first-quarter buzzer. His shot ended an 8-0 Sixers run and trimmed Toronto's lead to nine.

He remained involved during an 11-0 burst late in the third quarter, knocking down a three and setting up Walker for a basket. Walker then made a three early in the fourth before Simons scored on the Sixers' next possession.

When Toronto pulled within six with less than five minutes remaining, Simons answered with his fifth three-pointer.

Making those shots is the most obvious way Simons can help a roster already featuring James, Joel Embiid, Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown. He said the Sixers have not made him feel as though every opportunity must go in.

"They encourage us to keep shooting the ball," Simons said. "Knowing that a team has that much confidence in us to keep shooting makes the game that much easier. Whether you miss or make, as long as we get the right shots in the right moments, I think everybody can live with the misses and makes."

Simons spent seven seasons in Portland before playing last year with Boston and Chicago. His move to Philadelphia reunited him with Walker and Rayan Rupert, another former Blazer who joined the Sixers on a two-way contract during the offseason.

Knowing familiar faces awaited him helped Simons enter another new locker room.

"It made the transition seamless," he said. "Just being able to have some comfortability with guys that I played with for multiple years. It's been fun, kind of feeling like a little bit of old times. Me, Bari and Rups have been through a lot."

Walker's path to Philadelphia began a year earlier and required an additional step.

After playing 188 games over three seasons with Portland, Walker signed a two-way contract with the Sixers in July 2025. He earned a standard contract in February and has since pushed his way into the backup-center conversation despite standing 6-foot-7.

Walker believes some of the work he did on losing teams was easily missed.

"When I first got to the league, I was part of a rebuilding process," he said. "I just felt like some of the little things I did didn't always get noticed when you're losing. I told myself, if I ever got to be part of a winning organization, then I'd go out and expand my abilities."

Rebounding has made Walker difficult for Nick Nurse to overlook.

Walker credited his timing and strong hands to lessons passed down by his father, former NBA center Samaki Walker. Those instincts have become especially useful while the Sixers piece together their frontcourt behind Embiid.

"Rebounding is a skill I kind of honed in on," Walker said. "My dad was a good rebounder. He was known for his rebounding and his timing. I think it's just a gift that he transferred to me."

Walker has made it clear that he expected to earn a role this season.

"I don't know if I was delusional, but I always felt like I was going to play and I was going to be a part," he said. "I read something where they said it's a surprise, but if you were around me this summer, it wouldn't be a surprise. This is expected, and I just want to continue to get better."

The freedom Simons once had in Portland is no longer necessary. Walker does not need a high volume of touches, either. Both understand that playing beside the Sixers' stars requires shooting when open, moving the ball and handling the less glamorous work.

"You've got to find ways to impact the game multiple ways, even if you're not scoring," Simons said. "Whether it's cutting, making the right read and pass to Joel, being the right swing pass to VJ, boxing out on defense or just being in the right position."

Their opportunities will fluctuate. Simons may take nine shots one night and only a few the next. Walker's minutes will depend on matchups and the health of Philadelphia's frontcourt.

Winning has changed what they are being asked to do. Their three years together in Portland taught them why the adjustment is worthwhile.

"You feel appreciative of the times where you go into a situation where your role might be a little bit less, but you're winning," Simons said. "That's something you can't take for granted."

#2719. No. 25 LSU adds RJ Luis Jr., who briefly was under contract with 2 NBA teams, to its roster

LSU added RJ Luis Jr., who briefly spent time under contract with two NBA teams, to its roster on Tuesday, a day before the 25th-ranked Tigers' scheduled game against Louisiana-Monroe.

The move comes after LSU President Wade Rousse released a statement saying the university intended to comply with a district court ruling in Baton Rouge that said the NCAA and Southeastern Conference could not declare Luis ineligible or punish LSU for allowing Luis to play.

"LSU is committed to compliance with NCAA and SEC rules, but it is equally committed to obeying the orders of Louisiana's courts," Rousse said.

The SEC called LSU's decision "deeply concerning and disappointing."

"LSU's decision today to rely on its own interpretation of the court's order to justify its actions is inconsistent with the expectations and responsibilities of membership in the Southeastern Conference," the league said in a statement. "The conference will continue to uphold and defend the standards and policies established by its member universities."

Luis, the 2025 Big East player of the Year with St. John's, spent parts of the 2025 NBA offseason with the Utah Jazz and Boston Celtics. He now joins a team that has won its first four games and this week entered the AP Top 25 on the heels of victory on Saturday over then-No. 14 Gonzaga.

"LSU, by rostering RJ, did the right thing," Luis' lawyer, Tony Clayton, told The Associated Press, noting that Luis never played in a regular-season NBA game and that the NCAA routinely issues waivers allowing players from professional leagues overseas to play for college teams in the United States.

LSU has three such international players on its roster: centers Brice Dessert and Michael Ruzic and forward Saliou Niang.

"It would be fundamentally unfair to RJ Luis to not allow him to play college ball when you have so many similarly situated young men who are playing in college now," Clayton said.

State district judge Ronald R. Johnson in East Baton Rouge Parish issued an injunction against the NCAA in Luis' case on Aug. 24. But the SEC shortly afterward strengthened rules against athletes who'd signed NFL, NBA or WNBA contracts, adding enforcement provisions that included fines of up to 50% of a program's revenue and half-season suspensions for coaches.

Johnson subsequently ruled that the SEC could not punish LSU for allowing Luis to play, and that LSU could not cite SEC rules as a reason for keeping Luis off its roster.

In his statement, Rousse noted that Luis "secured his eligibility through a court order before the new SEC policy interpretation was announced. LSU supports the updated policy going forward, and we remain a proud and committed member of the SEC."

Rousse also noted that the SEC had confirmed to LSU that it would abide by the state court rulings in Luis' case.

LSU coach Will Wade, now in his second stint with the university that fired him in 2022 over NCAA recruiting violations, initially held off on placing Luis on the roster while the district court ruling was challenged in state appeals courts.

Last Friday, the Louisiana Supreme Court upheld Johnson's preliminary injunction against the NCAA, the SEC and LSU.

Whether Luis will remain on the roster for the entire season is unclear. That depends on how aggressively the NCAA and SEC continue to pursue the case, and the court's timeline.

Clayton, who declared after a previous favorable ruling in an eligibility case that justice was served to SEC Commissioner Greg Sankey "like a cold cup of gumbo," said he hopes the Luis matter can be settled amicably.

"I would love to sit down with Sankey and buy him a hot cup of gumbo," Clayton said. "We've got to move forward. We've got to put it behind us. I just hope they allow this kid to live his life."

#2718. Dan Campbell sends Lions clear celebration message after Aidan Hutchinson injury

With the Lions losing star edge rusher Aidan Hutchinson for the season in their 35-10 overseas win over the Patriots, head coach Dan Campbell stood firm on his stance regarding player celebrations.

Hutchinson's season came to an unfortunate end after a sack on Patriots quarterback Drake Maye in the first quarter; the five-year veteran landed awkwardly after jumping in the air in celebration and tore his ACL in the process.

During Tuesday's appearance on Detroit's 97.1 The Ticket, Campbell verified the defensive star's injury status and how it will affect an already depleted defense.

Detroit Lions defensive end Aidan Hutchinson (97) sits on the field after an injury during a game in Munich, Germany, Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. AP Photo/Matthias Schrader

"What a gut punch, just because … he's such a phenomenal player," Campbell said. "This guy's an impact player, but more importantly, he's an unbelievable teammate, and he's one of our captains.

"It just stinks, man. It hits you. And I hate it for him, man, because he was on a heater. What a hell of a year he was having. He's just improved every year and to come off the injury he had [in 2024]. But I also know this, man: this guy will overcome this and he will come back better than he was, too. It's just the way he is, it's the way he's wired, and he'll come out better for this."

Regarding whether the celebration caused the injury, Campbell played it off as a freak accident.

"No, I don't believe that," Campbell said. "I think what happened speaks for itself. We're not talking to 8-year-olds here. And I'll give you a story. I remember one time I made a big play and I freaking pumped my arm up and I'm pretty sure I tore my bicep, but I didn't tell anybody. I was just excited. I just pumped my arm. It's not like I — so if you say, 'Hey, don't pump your arm next time, make sure you stay stoic,' man, this game is built off of emotion. And if you have to contain yourself after a play is made … then you're really becoming something you're not."

Detroit Lions head coach Dan Campbell speaks at a press conference after an NFL football game in Munich, Germany, Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

The gesture was similar to the typical goal celebration in soccer that fans of FC Bayern Munich would see at Allianz Arena in Munich, Germany, where the latest European NFL matchup was held.

Lions fans remember the outcome vividly, as former linebacker Stephen Tulloch suffered the same fate as Hutchinson back in 2014 against the Green Bay Packers.

Hutchinson had 24 tackles on the season and six sacks before getting hurt.

This marks the defensive end's second season-ending injury in the span of three seasons.

The injury, Campbell said, was "one of those things that happened."

"I hate it, I know he hates it. But I mean, the number of moves that I've seen Hutch make and the number of plyometrics, explosive movements, the spins, the one leg lateral movements, lateral balances, I'm just like, it was one of those things, man," the coach said. "I hate it. I think we all understand what it is, and let's be smart, but no, I'm not gonna say, 'Nobody celebrates anymore.'"

The Lions went into the international matchup without middle linebacker Jack Campbell, who left Week 8's 19-15 win over the Minnesota Vikings on a stretcher.

Campbell is currently on injured reserve with a neck injury.

Celebrations have been a recurring topic in the NFL, and the Lions have been at the center of the conversation, from Amon-Ra St. Brown emulating President Donald Trump's dance to Jameson Williams getting a penalty for dry-humping the turf after a touchdown.

#2717. Widespread outages hit banks, air travel, the NSA and Apple, disrupting seemingly everything

Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton dismissed suspicions that malicious activity led to a series of outages at major tech firms and government agencies over the past week.

"This is not cyberattack, and this is not a coordinated effort by a nation state," he told CNBC's "Closing Bell" on Tuesday. "We've looked very closely."

It's been a tough few days for the technology world after a series of widespread disruptions brought different parts of life to a halt.

On Tuesday, an outage halted internal communications at the National Security Agency, and iPhone maker Apple confirmed that different products were either down or having issues.

The NSA pointed to "scheduled routine maintenance," while Apple cited "an internal update" as the reason behind its issues.

It marked four major system problems in four consecutive business days.

Microsoft's Outlook issues wreaked havoc on businesses on Thursday, and the Federal Aviation Administration grounded flights on Friday. On Monday, issues at JPMorgan Chase affected customer transactions, and an outage at United Airlines briefly delayed departing flights.

"When Microsoft goes down, the whole world comes to a standstill," said Raj Rajamani, CEO of JetStream Security. "That's a stark reminder of how deeply embedded these services and technologies are in our day-to-day life."

The disruptions have rattled consumers and led to a steady stream of memes on social media, as people look for a silver lining — or sinister plot — in the string of technology problems.

Experts say the real culprit behind this strange series of coincidences is much more benign.

"We're in the weird teenage phase of technology not quite maturing enough to know how to do this without disrupting services and so forth," said Tom Hegel, research head at SentinelOne.

With the help of artificial intelligence, businesses are patching more software vulnerabilities than ever before and updating archaic systems to prepare for swarms of attacks in the wake of high-profile incidents like Hugging Face.

That's leaving information technology and security teams strained and scrambling to keep up with heightened demand, said Sam Rubin, senior vice president of consulting and threat intelligence at Palo Alto Networks' Unit 42.

Amid this prolonged period of uncertainty, Rubin said businesses should prioritize updating their most exploitable systems and keeping operations underway when an outage hits.

"You need to think about how you continue to operate in the face of one of your IT systems going down," he said.

Clayton told CNBC that routine upgrades are commonplace at any organization and called for a nationwide digital infrastructure upgrade.

Last week, millions of users lost access to email in a fiasco that Microsoft eventually said was the result of an undetected bug that was lurking since July.

The FAA, which is still looking into the root cause of its system failure, pointed in part to a router upgrade in southern Arkansas overseen by government contractor L3Harris Technologies that overwhelmed the agency's telecommunications network.

JPMorgan said longer-than-usual service times on Monday stemmed from a software bug in on-premise hardware systems.

Agencies and businesses have ruled out cyberattacks, but the routine patching alibi hasn't satisfied some skeptics, and lawmakers are calling for more transparency.

Sen. Mark Warner, D-Va., on Tuesday used the recent incidents to make the case for an AI safety bill. House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., acknowledged that it's "too premature" to conclude malicious activity but called for a congressional briefing.

"I think it's time for the administration to come up to the Hill and explain themselves so we can be informed about the steps that need to be taken and we can inform the people of the United States of America," he said during a press conference on Tuesday.

#2716. Observations after Brown-Maxey duo scores 58 points in Sixers' win without Embiid

Observations after Brown-Maxey duo scores 58 points in Sixers' win without Embiid originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Jaylen Brown-Tyrese Maxey duo starred, LeBron James reached another milestone, and the Sixers earned a win without Joel Embiid on Sunday afternoon.

They improved to 11-3 on the season with a 131-124 victory over the Pacers at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Brown scored 31 points and Maxey posted 27.

LeBron James had 15 points and 11 assists. He passed Jason Kidd on Sunday for third in NBA history with 12,092 assists.

Pascal Siakam scored 31 points for the 5-9 Pacers.

On the first day of a back-to-back, the Sixers were down Embiid (right foot plantar fascia injury management), Dominick Barlow (lower back stress reaction), Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear). The Pacers' injured players included Tyrese Haliburton and Andrew Nembhard.

The Sixers will face the Nets on Monday night in Brooklyn. Here are observations on their win over the Pacers:

Jabari Walker started for the first time this season, opening the game at center. He's been in the Sixers' rotation the past five games since Bona's injury.

Walker set up the Sixers' first basket, swiping a steal and then tossing the ball ahead to Maxey for a fast-break dunk. Maxey and Brown both began well and combined to score all of the Sixers' first 15 points.

Though he conceded five inches to 7-foot Pacers center Ivica Zubac, Walker was solid in his first stint. He got an early post stop on Zubac and then snatched a strong defensive rebound.

Dean Wade was next up for the Sixers at the five. He sunk a three-pointer on his first shot to give the team a 20-16 lead.

Walker ended up playing 25 minutes and posting 11 points and seven rebounds. Going back to the preseason, his stock has steadily risen.

"Here we are again after another game saying Jabari played very well," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "He plays hard, he rebounds, he's got another level of confidence with his shooting this year.

"It's kind of wash, rinse and repeat again with him (today). He was great. … Jabari went in there and battled (Zubac). I thought he certainly matched him, if not outplayed him, which is a heck of a job."

Wade had 12 points and seven boards in 35 minutes. Overall, the Sixers' lack of healthy conventional centers is still a clear deficiency. Of course, everything should be simpler once Embiid is back in action.

The Sixers' defense did not have a sparkling start. The Pacers tallied 35 points in the first quarter and shot 62.5 percent from the field.

Brown helped the Sixers avoid a substantial deficit by draining all sorts of jumpers. He rarely appears bothered by close contests. Brown moved to 7 for 9 from the floor when he knocked down a three early in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope's triple knotted the contest up at 37-all.

Nurse needed to stray from his Plan A rotation when VJ Edgecombe picked up his third foul with 9:56 left in the second quarter. Labaron Philon Jr., who got a game with the G League's Delaware Blue Coats on Friday night, subbed in.

For a rookie guard, Philon has looked comfortable and confident on offense in most of his NBA appearances. He swished a long-range jumper in the second quarter and saw a floater rim out.

Brown reached the 20-point mark and put the Sixers up one with a slam assisted by James late in the second quarter.

He ran into serious foul trouble in the third quarter. Brown was whistled for his fourth foul with 9:53 left in the period for an off-arm shove on Aaron Nesmith. Wade entered and Brown exited.

The Sixers' slim lead didn't slip away. Walker nailed threes on consecutive possessions and Maxey jammed in a dunk.

Soon after, Tacko Fall came in for his first meaningful minutes as a Sixer. The 7-foot-6 two-way contract player grabbed an offensive board off a missed Edgecombe free throw, which drew one of the day's loudest cheers. With Fall on the floor, Maxey and James connected on an alley-oop that extended the Sixers' lead to 82-73.

When the Sixers tried to sit in a zone, Fall was called for a defensive three-second violation. Whatever the scheme, most of Fall's defensive work will come around the paint. He certainly has exceptional physical traits as a rim protector and is not nimble on the perimeter.

Fall logged five scoreless third-quarter minutes and recorded three rebounds. The Sixers outscored Indiana by a point with Fall on the court. Given Fall is occupying a (two-way) roster slot and the Sixers are so depleted at center, it made sense to see a bit of what he can do outside of garbage time. While five minutes isn't enough for any grand conclusions, it's very safe to say Fall is a unique player.

"I'm 7-6, so I can change the game in so many ways," Fall said. "Offensively, setting screens, making sure guys get open, being an easy target for lobs. Defensively, I feel like I've always been an elite rim protector and I've worked on my body a lot to be able to move a lot better."

Nurse praised Fall's outing, saying he "gave us some great minutes and a nice lift."

Fall is appreciative of his first NBA opportunity since the 2021-22 season.

"I've worked very hard," he said. "God has brought me back here and I'm thankful. And I'm ready. Whenever my name is called, I'm going to go out there and do my job."

The Sixers built their advantage to 102-87 with a Brown three early in the fourth quarter. They couldn't cruise to a blowout, though.

Siakam kept hurting the Sixers as a driver and had a highly efficient fourth quarter, scoring 19 points on 7-for-9 shooting. Quenton Jackson's layup trimmed Indiana's deficit to 119-115.

The Sixers ultimately held off the Pacers' push.

Caldwell-Pope canned a three, Wade threw down an and-one dunk, and James checked off his milestone assist with a late dish to Edgecombe for a layup.

#2715. Wales' Nations League campaign boosts Bellamy's belief

Craig Bellamy kept coming back to one word. Belief.

His Wales side had just lost a fifth game out of six to finish bottom of their Nations League group. Bellamy would have been forgiven for feeling a little bruised.

But there are ways of losing and ways of finishing bottom.

Wales were fearless against Portugal, exhilarating in attack and proving they can compete at the highest level of this competition, even as they were beaten 3-2 in Cardiff.

And while they were already consigned to the foot of the Group A4 table, Wales' previous performances mean they at least have the back-up of a relegation play-off in March to preserve their top-flight status.

"Belief's the biggest one," Bellamy said when he was asked what he and his team had taken from this campaign.

"Not just this game, but in general. We are beyond close to this level. We can more than compete at this level.

"We demand and want to expect to be. Getting into League A is big for us, but also staying in A is going to allow us to develop.

"You can only develop and improve by playing the best teams, and I felt we've taken another shift."

Portugal hold off bold Wales in five-goal thriller

Former Wales captain Craig Bellamy was appointed head coach in July 2024 [Huw Evans Picture Agency]

Wales have certainly fared better than they did when they were last in the top tier of the Nations League, when they finished with one point from six games and were relegated.

Victory against World Cup quarter-finalists Norway last month was their first at this level and, had they been more clinical in Oslo on Saturday, they might have got a second win of the campaign.

Ultimately, Wales' inability to take their chances – combined with sloppy defensive mistakes – meant their results did not match their performances.

And the reality is that they were up against stellar opposition, whose vastly superior resources showed with their greater ruthlessness at both ends of the pitch.

"If there's anything you would have learned from these games, then you see killers. That's why they're at the top," Bellamy added.

"I know people look at results. I'm fully aware of the business. This is all I know. But I think when you look at reality as well, and you look at our performances during this group, I couldn't ask for more.

"I feel we're closer than probably maybe the players thought they were. It's fine margins.

"But I feel the biggest word out of this now has been belief, really, for me."

Wales had the best record of the four teams who finished bottom of their top-flight groups, meaning they have a second chance to avoid relegation in the form of a two-leg play-off in March.

At Friday's draw in Switzerland, Bellamy's side will discover whether they will face Slovenia, Hungary, Republic of Ireland or Bosnia-Herzegovina, who beat them in a World Cup play-off earlier this year.

Those teams all finished second in their League B groups and, as the League A team, Wales will have home advantage in the second leg.

"Going into the next one, we have to be in this group," said Bellamy. "If we want to develop as a football nation and keep going forward, we have to be playing against the best teams. It's only going to improve you.

"I know we can go back to League B... It's about playing the best teams. That's how we're going to get better. I honestly believe it."

Bellamy, who has a contract running to 2028, said: "I won't be here for the next Nations League. I won't be the manager then. So I'm doing it for the next one, but I'm also doing it for the players.

"We've got to. That's how we're going to be the best version of us and keep improving."

#2713. Inside Sixers-Nets: How Philly can finish a back-to-back sweep in Brooklyn

Inside Sixers-Nets: How Philly can finish a back-to-back sweep in Brooklyn originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers will arrive in Brooklyn with one victory already secured on their back-to-back and a different combination of stars likely available for the second.

Philadelphia improved to 11-3 with a 131-124 win over the Pacers on Sunday. Jaylen Brown scored a season-high 31 points, Tyrese Maxey added 27 and LeBron James recorded 11 assists to move past Jason Kidd for third in NBA history.

James has not played both ends of a back-to-back this season, making it reasonable to expect him to sit Monday night. Joel Embiid, meanwhile, rested Sunday and is expected to return against Brooklyn. The Sixers had not submitted their official injury report as of Monday morning.

The Nets enter at 3-9, but this is not the same roster that finished 20-62 last season. Before the Sixers attempt to win twice in two nights, here is a closer look at what has changed in Brooklyn and where Monday's matchup could turn.

Nic Claxton is now in Chicago. Julius Randle arrived from Minnesota as the central piece of a four-team trade. Keon Ellis and Moe Wagner joined in free agency, while Michael Porter Jr. remains one of Brooklyn's primary scoring options.

Porter is available after missing Saturday's game for personal reasons. He will give the Nets another shooter after Brooklyn struggled badly without him in a 129-110 loss to Memphis.

The Nets' rotation also includes a large group of young players. Egor Dëmin, Nolan Traore and Mikel Brown Jr. have all received minutes in the backcourt, while Noah Clowney, Danny Wolf and Day'Ron Sharpe have played in the frontcourt.

The combination has produced uneven results. Brooklyn averages 110.2 points and allows 116.4 per game. Its minus-6.3 net rating ranks 23rd in the league.

Brooklyn has shown its offensive ceiling. The Nets scored 145 points in a 34-point win over New Orleans last week. Three nights later, they fell behind Memphis by 26 and lost by 19.

Jordi Fernandez has been direct about the difference between the team he sees in practice and the one that has appeared in games.

"We practice better than we play," the Nets head coach said after Saturday's loss. "It's a frustrating sign because you believe you're better."

The Nets did not merely miss shots against Memphis. Their problems began with their approach.

"We didn't punch first," Randle said. "Just organization. I think maybe just trying too hard, playing a little unorganized, stuff like that. And then just attention to detail. I think we've got a lapse of focus sometimes."

Fernandez was even more direct when discussing his young guards.

"They've got to grow fast," he said. "Being 20 is not an excuse."

That inexperience has appeared in Brooklyn's ball control. The Nets' offensive turnover rate ranks 28th in the league.

They have been much more disruptive on the other end. Brooklyn has forced turnovers on 13.9 percent of its opponents' possessions, tied for seventh.

The Sixers forced 10 Indiana turnovers and converted them into 19 points Sunday. Philadelphia committed 13 total turnovers while playing at the quick tempo it has emphasized since training camp.

Brooklyn has enough length and athleticism to disrupt the Sixers. It has not consistently maintained its own organization after creating that pressure.

Brooklyn has allowed 14.2 three-pointers per game, the eighth-most in the NBA. Opponents have posted a 55.2 effective field-goal percentage against the Nets.

That presents another opportunity for a Sixers team receiving strong outside shooting from several role players.

Anfernee Simons ranks third in the league with 33 three-pointers off the bench and is shooting 41.8 percent from deep. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope has made 46.5 percent of his threes, while Dean Wade is at 41.4 percent.

Maxey made five threes against Indiana and Brown added three. Jabari Walker also continued his strong start by making three of his six attempts from deep.

Those contributions matter on the second night of a back-to-back, especially if James sits. Philadelphia should not need Maxey and Brown to create every possession from a standstill if its shooters continue punishing open space.

Embiid's expected return would change the shape of Philadelphia's offense after James served as its primary organizer Sunday.

Brooklyn no longer has Claxton to use against Embiid. Sharpe has moved into a larger role and is coming off 16 points and 11 rebounds in Memphis. Wagner, Clowney and Randle give Fernandez several other bodies to try, but none presents the same defensive profile as Claxton.

Sharpe's work on the glass deserves attention. The Sixers have survived significant rebounding deficits this season, but allowing Brooklyn extra possessions would give Porter and Randle more chances to settle into the game.

On the other end, Embiid should have opportunities to force Brooklyn's frontcourt into difficult decisions. Sending help would create openings for Philadelphia's shooters. Defending him individually would leave Sharpe, Wagner and Clowney with a demanding assignment.

The Sixers have already won three games without James. Monday could require them to do it again, this time with Embiid returning as the center of the offense.

Brooklyn's record suggests a favorable matchup. Its shooting and young talent make it less comfortable than the standings indicate.

The Sixers handled the first half of their back-to-back by producing a season-high 131 points. Finishing the sweep will require them to adjust to a different available roster while preventing an inconsistent Nets team from finding one of its explosive nights.

#2712. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2708. Through 10 weeks, the AFC playoff field is basically set

The 2021 expansion of the regular season to 17 games has given the bad teams more time to separate from the good teams. Which makes it easier for the eventual playoff teams to declare themselves.

This year, the seven AFC playoff teams have essentially declared themselves after only 10 weeks of games.

The most important developments in that regard were these: (1) the Browns beating the Texans; (2) the Ravens losing to the Chargers; and (3) the Patriots losing badly to the Lions — and losing quarterback Drake Maye, possibly for the rest of the season.

Already, we know that the Bills, Jaguars, Bengals, Broncos, and Chiefs are essentially locks to win tickets to the postseason party. The Steelers, despite being outclassed by the Bengals, have enough winnable games remaining to land a wild-card berth.

The only spot up for grabs is the seven seed. And the Browns are now in the driver’s seat.

Already, four AFC teams are dead in the water: the 2-8 Jets, the 1-8 Dolphins, the 1-8 Titans, and the 1-8 Chargers. The 3-7 Colts barely have a pulse, and it won’t last long. That’s five of 16 teams, with a fork already stuck in a spot where forks aren’t supposed to go. And the 3-6 Texans aren’t far behind.

The remaining contenders for the last spot in the playoff tree are the 6-4 Browns, the 5-5 Patriots, the 4-6 Raiders, and the 4-6 Ravens. New England could be in the process of imploding. The Raiders are 1-6 over their last seven games, and they’re now starting a rookie quarterback. The Ravens have lost five in a row.

As a result, there’s currently not much drama as to who the seven AFC playoff teams will be.

The good news is that the top of the seven-team hierarchy remains undecided — and it could stay that way for a while. The Bills, Jaguars, and Bengals stand apart from the rest. The Broncos and Chiefs remain in the mix. (The Steelers seem to be destined for another one-and-done postseason appearance, with a road game looming to Buffalo, Jacksonville, Cincinnati, or Denver/Kansas City.)

The bad news, and the inescapable reality, is that the have-nots have become crystal clear. With eight weeks of the season left.

For a league that loves parity, there isn’t much of it when it comes to the line separating the playoff teams from the wait-til-next-year franchises.

#2707. Stephen Curry assumed last summer's contract extension might be last one

How much the Golden State Warriors still rely on Stephen Curry has been painfully obvious since he went down with an oblique abdominal tear: 0-3, with the losses by a combined 66 points, and the Warriors failed to score 100 points in the last two.

Even before this injury, last season's knee issues had Curry being realistic about his future when speaking with Anthony Slater of ESPN for a must-read story. Curry signed a two-year, $115.9 million extension last summer that could take him for two seasons beyond this one (the second of those years is a player option).

"I assumed that it would probably be one of, if not the last, contract I signed. I'm not afraid to say it. But maybe I'll surprise myself and want to keep going."

Curry has accepted where he is in his career. He still wants to win (which is why he didn't take a max extension) and says he would take on a smaller role if that's what helps the team. Curry also discusses how he had to alter his intense off-season workout routine to give his body more time to recover — he can't just push through everything anymore.

None of that has shown on the court this season, as Curry has been spectacular, averaging 27.8 points per game while shooting 45.7% from 3-point range. He's doing that in reduced minutes — coach Steve Kerr is trying to keep him at around 28 minutes a night — and with less movement, to ease things on those older knees.

None of that diminishes Curry's incredible gravity, which draws defenders and opens things up for rookie sensation Yaxel Landeborg to get off to a fast start. With Curry out the past couple of games and defenses focused on him, things have been tougher for Landeborg. Things will get easier for Curry and the Warriors when Jimmy Butler (torn ACL) and Moses Moody (ruptured patellar tendon) return from injuries, expected later this season. Until then, the Warriors have to find a way to stay in the postseason mix in a deep West.

Curry, for his part, is still in this to win it, but he may be taking more time to stop and smell the roses, he told ESPN.

"I want to appreciate what's happening right now because I don't want to just go through the motions," Curry said. "Then, it passes you by."

#2706. Oscar Piastri set for grid penalty at F1 Las Vegas GP

Piastri was already on the limit of four internal combustion engines for the year, but will need a fifth Mercedes V6 engine to finish the 2026 season.

As it is the first time he is exceeding his ICE allowance, the Australian will be docked 10 places on Las Vegas' starting grid.

"Oscar will take a new Internal Combustion Engine in Las Vegas after a reliability issue," the team said in its event preview. "As it is his fifth of the season, he will take a 10-place grid penalty, but he has a good chance to recover positions with overtaking in Saturday's race."

McLaren also confirmed it will take Mercedes' upgraded turbocharger, which the manufacturer developed under the FIA's so-called ADUO catch-up mechanism.

"Both cars, as with the other HPP-powered cars, will be fitted with a new ADUO-enabled turbo specification: the expected performance benefit is relatively small," McLaren noted.

Oscar Piastri, McLaren, Alexander Albon, Williams

The Mercedes works team will trial the new turbo on newly crowned world champion Kimi Antonelli's car, while George Russell will stay on the original specification to avoid a grid drop, as Russell had already reached the limit of his allowance.

"We will have the ADUO enabled turbo specification available from this weekend," said Mercedes F1 chief Toto Wolff. "This is primarily about proving out the development in race-weekend conditions, with the expected performance benefit relatively small.

#2704. Inside the Cowboys historic defensive collapse against the 49ers

The Dallas Cowboys lost a game to the San Francisco 49ers. That's not a shocker to anyone, as this Cowboys team has struggled all season, and the 49ers are the best team they've played so far, and by a good margin. Looking at their opponents, San Francisco is the only offense the Cowboys have faced this year that is ranked in the top eight in scoring, now averaging over 31 points a game. In fact, the Cowboys haven't fared well against offenses that average over 22 points a game.

They've played five teams that average more than 22 points/game. Their record against those teams is 1-4.

They've played five teams that average fewer than 22 points/game. Their record against those teams is 3-2.

Clearly, playing decent offenses is not ideal for this Cowboys defense, so when they faced the NFC's highest-scoring offense, it was a huge problem for a unit that has struggled immensely all season. Even so, it shouldn't have been that bad. The defense gave up 584 total yards on the day. It was the most yards they have ever given up at home during their 67-year existence. The most ever is never good.

The frustrating part about this game is that when you take a closer look, it's easy to tell that the Cowboys never had a shot. The defense was just outmatched. It was like playing one of those games where you play against an older sibling, where they might toy around with you for a bit, only to flatten you when it's time to get serious.

The 49ers did that to the Cowboys' defense. That doesn't mean they were playing opossum at any point during the game. It's just that whenever the 49ers found themselves in a crunch, they always had a go-to play where they knew they could take advantage of the Cowboys' vulnerabilities.

The Cowboys had several defensive breakdowns in the game, with multiple third-down letdowns and explosive plays. One of the most embarrassing plays came on the Deebo Samuel reverse play in the third quarter. When fullback Kyle Juszczyk went in motion, the entire defense was lured into a trance. Follow the Juice because that's where the action goes most of the time. Only this time, the Juice wasn't worth the squeeze.

Sadly, the entire defense took the bait. It was crazy to see how many defenders continued chasing running back Kaelon Black. That left an entourage of blockers out in front of Samuel, leaving very little chance for the Cowboys to make a stop. Malik Hooker and Shemar James were the first to figure it out, but they were so far out of position that by the time they caught up, they were left gambling and taking poor angles. Cornerback Joey Porter Jr. was the last hope. He's not the greatest tackler as it is, but he's even worse when he's washed out by multiple blockers.

In our game preview article, we mentioned that "Shanahanigans" would be an issue due to the Cowboys' over-pursuing nature, and right on cue, they obliged. It was one of the poorest-defended reverse plays we've seen.

Mental lapses are common for this defense, but more frustrating is when their effort is so lackadaisical. The touchdown run by the rookie Black was horrible for different reasons. It starts with Juice looping around with banana motion. He and wide receiver Jordan Watkins take on the outside defenders, leaving a nice hole for Black to run through. With no defenders to impede his path, Black gets a full head of steam and commits some terrible Big 10 rookie-on-rookie crime by running over Caleb Downs.

Yes, that's the Cowboys' top draft pick being leveled, but in fairness to Downs, he squared up well. It's just hard to defy physics when the running back comes plowing through with that much force.

The rest of the Cowboys' defense is sealed off from clever run-blocking that creeps to the second level. Cornerback Cobie Durant has one final chance to bring him down, but he gets run over like he's not even there. It wouldn't be a Cowboys game without a humiliating run-stop attempt against a team's backup running back.

Getting their playmakers in space was the name of the game for the 49ers, and they did it often. They just meandered off to one side, got the defense to cha-cha-cha in that direction, and then boom, sealed off! On the quick pass to Samuel, the Cowboys' defense was so out of position that their lone, helpless cornerback wasn't even able to get a hand on him.

And then there were the two long touchdown passes to Christian McCaffrey. To be fair, McCaffrey is a handful all of the time, so game planning for him isn't easy. The first touchdown catch was especially hard because the defense actually played well for 90% of the play. It was just hanging on for that last 10% that got them in trouble. McCaffrey was a sneaky sneakerton and, at the last second, leaked out into the secondary, and as soon as Brock Purdy saw that, it was over.

The second touchdown was a harder pill to swallow for a couple of reasons. First, it essentially iced the game. Any hopes of a comeback were crushed. Second, in the defense's biggest moment, they were once again escorted out of the play. It was a simple play for the 49ers, with all their receivers basically running away from where McCaffrey was, taking the defense with them. It was like Moses parting the Red Sea, only Kyle Shanahan was holding the staff, and it was the Cowboys' defense that opened up for the easiest pitch-and-catch you'll ever see.

The Cowboys' defense has been a problem all season, and the 49ers exploited every element of it. The same things keep happening, and they aren't showing any sign of improvement. And it's not going to get any easier with four of their final five games against offenses averaging over 22 points a game. This defense has fallen into troubling times with very little chance of escape.

#2703. Top-ranked Florida tops Notre Dame 86-78 despite losing star point guard Boogie Fland

LAS VEGAS (AP) — Urban Klavzar scored 24 points off the bench and No. 1 Florida beat Notre Dame 86-78 on Tuesday in the opening round of the Players Era 8 tournament despite losing Boogie Fland to an injury in the first half.

Fland, Florida's starting point guard who missed the previous game against Merrimack with a right shoulder injury, left after a fall. He went scoreless and had one assist in eight minutes.

#2702. The prediction markets are questioning Mike Vrabel's job security, again

During the offseason, the leading prediction markets were calling into question the future of Patriots coach Mike Vrabel. At one point, Kalshi had his chances of being the head coach as of Week 1 at only 62.2 percent.

Now that the Patriots have suffered a pair of embarrassing losses, the possibility of Vrabel not being the head coach of the Patriots after the season is creeping up again.

On Kalshi’s master list of coaches to be fired by March 2027, Vrabel is at nine percent. On Polymarket, the specific question of whether Vrabel will be out by December 31, 2026 currently stands at 13 percent.

Since the regular season doesn’t end until January 10, the notion that he’s regarded as having a 13-percent chance of being fired by December 31 suggests that, if the date were shifted to Monday, January 11, the number would be higher.

Could it happen, one year after a Super Bowl berth in Vrabel’s first season with the team? The chances seem to be much lower than 13 percent, on the surface. However, there’s a weird vibe coming from the team right now, as illustrated by some of the things Vrabel said both after Sunday’s 35-10 loss to the Lions and on Monday.

It’s fair to ask whether Vrabel has lost his touch. It’s fair to wonder whether his offseason entanglement has eroded some of his authority within the locker room.

Coaches routinely rail against distractions. Vrabel created a significant one that crossed over into mainstream news. While plenty of players would likely shrug at the situation, some may see hypocrisy. Some may have become exhausted by the endless questions from family and friends about the situation.

The failure of any team in the NFL bubbles over from a complex cauldron of factors. The players may not be good enough. The preparation may not be effective enough. Bad luck plays a role.

Still, the coach needs to be able to press the buttons of the players in order to get the most out of them. And it doesn’t take much to tune someone out — even a football coach.

Those are the critical questions. Has a critical mass of players tuned him out? Are they not listening to him? Are they not responding to him?

Ultimately, that’s something for ownership to monitor and to diagnose. While the organizational play was to sharply turn the page and move on from the scandal, that only works if the team wins. With the Patriots at 5-5 — and with the defense surrendering 881 yards in losses to the Packers and Lions and being outgained by Detroit, 494 yards to 151 — it’s important to get to the “why?” residing at the center of the “what the f—k?”

The Patriots still have seven games to play. Four of them are against the 8-1 Bills, the 7-2 Vikings, the 7-2 Chiefs, and the 7-2 Broncos. And they may not have Maye for any of them.

The chances of a playoff berth are slipping away. A winning record may not happen, either. When the season ends, the powers-that-be will have to decide whether they believe that things will be any better in 2027.

#2700. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

#2699. Nick Bosa’s shocking drought comes with a 49ers silver lining

The San Francisco 49ers hold an 8-1 record, the best in the NFC, and yet they still haven't gotten quite the same type of production out of Nick Bosa that they're accustomed to.

NBC Sports Bay Area 49ers insider Matt Maiocco was asked if Bosa would ever be productive again and provided his insight on why he's a key contributor to the 49ers' pass rush.

"Nick Bosa does not have a sack in the four games he has played this season. Never before has he gone four consecutive games in the same season without a sack since coming into the NFL in 2019," Maiocco said.

San Francisco 49ers edge rusher Nick Bosa during team warmups at AT&T Stadium. Getty Images

Since Week 2 of last year, Bosa has missed an extensive amount of time dealing with a torn ACL last season and a calf injury this year.

Maiocco added that Bosa not being able to participate in training camp this past summer put him behind the rest of his teammates, and then when he was cleared to play again, he played in two games, and then strained his calf.

Bosa returned two weeks ago against the Raiders and recorded one tackle. He played 57 snaps against the Cowboys last Sunday, finishing without a tackle but registering a quarterback hit.

Nick Bosa coming out of the tunnel before an NFL game against the Cowboys. Getty Images

Maiocco added that Bosa is still winning despite the longest drought of his career.

"Bosa leads the 49ers' defensive line with a pass-rush win rate of 16.7 percent. That is down from past seasons, when he typically is around a 20 percent figure," Maiocco said.

On Monday, 49ers head coach Kyle Shanahan appeared on a conference call that included Maiocco, and he was asked if he was worried about Bosa and told reporters, "I'm not worried about Nick."

"Nick is going to get better each week, and he'll eventually get there," Shanahan said.

While San Francisco is waiting for Bosa to reform himself, the 49ers defense can be patient.

Nick Bosa attempting to tackle Raiders tight end Michael Mayer. Getty Images

The 49ers rank second in the NFL in run defense, allowing 82.6 rushing yards per game. They're also giving up an average of 21.8 points per game.

Their pass rush has been inconsistent, especially Sunday, when the 49ers managed just one sack of Cowboys quarterback Dak Prescott, who threw for 343 yards and five touchdowns.

The 49ers are tied with the Eagles for 30th in the NFL with 16 sacks, underscoring their struggles to get after opposing quarterbacks.

Perhaps if Bosa can get back to his old self soon, he could give the 49ers' pass rush the jolt that they desperately need.

#2698. All four NFC South teams are terrible at home, and one will get a home playoff game

The NFL’s playoff seeding procedures guarantee that an NFC South team will host a playoff game. It may prove to be the easiest place to play that any road playoff team has ever visited.

All four NFC South teams are terrible at home this season: The first-place Panthers are 1-3 at home. The Buccaneers are 1-4. The Saints and Falcons are both 1-5. Add it up, and the four NFC South teams are a combined 4-17 at home.

But someone has to win the NFC South, and that “someone” will be at home during the wild card round of the playoffs, almost certainly playing some second-place team that finished the regular season with a much better record. Most likely, the NFC South winner will be the No. 4 seed, and the second-place finisher in the NFC North will be the No. 5 seed.

Currently, the NFC North features a three-way tie among the Lions, Vikings and Bears at 7-2. For those three teams, coming in second in their division and starting the playoffs on the road against the NFC South winner might actually be a better path to the Super Bowl than winning the division and starting the playoffs at home against a wild card team, likely an NFC North rival or the Rams or Seahawks.

The major prize to earn in the regular season is the No. 1 seed, which comes with a first-round bye and home-field advantage. For an NFC contender that falls short of the No. 1 seed, the best bet might be the No. 5 seed, and an easy place to play in the first round of the playoffs.

The project is partnered with Prodrive.
